# **About this book**

This is a digital copy of a work that has been preserved for generations on library shelves before being carefully scanned by Google as part of a project aimed at allowing internet users to discover the entirety of the world's literary heritage online.

Since this book is relatively old, it is no longer protected by copyright law and now belongs to the public domain. The expression "belonging to the public domain" means that the book in question has never been subject to copyright or that its legal rights have expired. The requirements for a book to fall into the public domain vary from one country to another. Public domain books are so many links to the past. They are witnesses to the richness of our history, our cultural heritage, and human knowledge, and are all too often difficult for the public to access.

The footnotes and other annotations in the margins of the text present in the original volume are included in this file, as a memory of the long journey traveled by the work from the publishing house, through the library, to finally find itself in your hands.

# **Usage guidelines**

Google is proud to work in partnership with libraries to digitize works belonging to the public domain and thus make them accessible to everyone. These books are indeed the property of all, and we are simply the guardians of this heritage. However, this is an expensive project. Consequently, and with a view to continuing the dissemination of these inexhaustible resources, we have taken the necessary steps to prevent potential abuses by third-party commercial sites, notably by establishing technical constraints regarding automated queries.

We also ask you to:

+ *Do not use the files for commercial purposes* We designed the Google Book Search program for use by individuals. We therefore ask you to use these files only for personal purposes. They cannot, in fact, be employed for any commercial goal whatsoever.

+ *Do not perform automated queries* Do not send any automated queries of any kind to the Google system. If you are conducting research concerning translation software, optical character recognition <note>OCR</note>, or any other field requiring large quantities of text, please do not hesitate to contact us. For the realization of this type of work, we encourage the use of works and documents belonging to the public domain and would be happy to be of assistance.

+ *Do not remove the attribution* The Google watermark contained in each file is essential for informing internet users of our project and allowing them to access more documents through the Google Book Search Program. Do not remove it under any circumstances.

+ *Stay within the law* Whatever use you plan to make of the files, remember that it is your responsibility to ensure you respect the law. If a work belongs to the American public domain, do not assume that the same applies in other countries. The legal duration of a book's copyright varies from one country to another. We are therefore not in a position to list which works are authorized for use and which are not. Do not believe that the simple fact of displaying a book on Google Book Search means that it can be used in any way whatsoever throughout the entire world. The penalties to which you might be exposed in the event of copyright violation can be severe.

# **About the Google Book Search service**

By promoting research and access to an increasing number of books available in many languages, including English <note>original: "français"</note>, Google wishes to contribute to promoting cultural diversity through Google Book Search. Indeed, the Google Book Search Program allows internet users to discover the world's literary heritage, while helping authors and publishers to broaden their audience. You can perform online searches in the full text of this work at the address <insert>http://books.google.com</insert>

## TO MY LORD
# PIERRE SCARRON,
## BISHOP AND PRINCE
### OF GRENOBLE, &c. <note>Pierre Scarron (1570–1667) was a significant ecclesiastical figure and the uncle of the famous poet Paul Scarron.</note>

*MY LORD, Since this composition is a general remedy, and a public medicine against all sorts of afflictions, it must be approved that I prescribe its use under the authority of your Name, and that I seek credit for it through the esteem you shall have for its merit. After the illustrious testimony of **Theodoret** <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–457 AD), an influential theologian and Bishop in Syria</note>, who can be ignorant of the fact that **Bishops** are the **Physicians** of the human race, and that it belongs to them—following the example of our charitable **Samaritan**—to mix the wine with the oil? That great Man <note>Referring to the prophet Elijah</note>, who left us such a beautiful idea of zeal in the ancient Law, and whom God reserves to assist at the final agonies of nature, never opened his mouth without a miracle; since, according to the observation of the **Hebrews**, all the words of **Elijah** were salutary medicines. And what do the **Prelates** <term>Prelates: high-ranking clergy members, such as bishops, who exercise authority</term> do in*

<meta>This is the opening page of the preface, introducing the historical and familial background of the author Boethius.</meta>

# CLARIFICATION
## necessary for the understanding of
## this Work.

It is important to know the character of Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), a Roman senator and philosopher</note> and the strength of his mind, in order to render the esteem due to his productions; not that it is necessary to borrow the panegyrics that the Reverend Father Caussin <note>Nicolas Caussin (1583–1651), a Jesuit hagiographer and confessor to Louis XIII</note> made for him in his *The Statesman* <note>original: "Homme d'Estat"</note>; nor that one is obliged to hold all the noble thoughts of that author to grasp the true idea of this excellent Philosopher. It is enough to know that he was descended from those Manlii <note>The Manlia gens, one of the oldest patrician families in Rome</note> who alone prevented the Gauls from triumphing entirely over the Capitol, and who drew the illustrious name of "Torquatus" from the chains <note>referring to the *torques* or neck-ring taken from a defeated Gaul</note> that their courage prepared for the Romans. This race was so fertile in Heroes that for the space of a thousand years it gave Consuls to the Empire; and if any member did not possess that great dignity, he was nevertheless judged worthy of it. Boethius, whom Ennodius <note>Magnus Felix Ennodius, a bishop and poet of the 5th century</note> calls **THE VEIN OF THE PURPLES** <note>original: "LA VEINE DES POVRPRES," a metaphor for his noble imperial lineage</note>, held this honor three times, and

->merited it<-

<summary>This preface introduces the philosopher Boethius by highlighting his noble lineage from the Roman Manlii family and his high political standing as a three-time Consul.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, Father Caussin, Manlii family, Torquatus, Ennodius, Roman Empire, Consulship, nobility</keywords>

...he deserved it all his life. Theodoric, King of the
Goths, recognizing his rare qualities, made him the

in such continuous affairs that it seemed he
had not enough leisure even to breathe. This
great employment, which was but a diversion
to his mind, did not prevent him from
devoting good hours to the composition
of many Writings, which earned him the
name of the "last of the learned" according to Lorenzo
Valla <note>Lorenzo Valla (1407–1457) was a famous Italian humanist and critic who praised Boethius's Latinity.</note>. There is hardly a subject in which
he has not shown his proficiency. The New
Academy <note>The "New Academy" refers to the skeptical phase of the Platonic school, though Boethius is more often associated with Neoplatonism.</note> nevertheless possessed his
best studies, although he had a perfect
understanding of the other Sects, particularly
that of Plato, whom he promised
to reconcile with Aristotle, if death had not
prevented his design. This great man,
having too much brilliance not to cause pain
to the eyes of envy, found many enemies
to his virtue. Trigilla, Conigastus, and Cyprian,
who held the greatest share in the
affairs of Theodoric, judged well that,
not being able to have him as an accomplice in their designs,
they ought to fear him as a censor
of their conduct. The liberty he took to
constrain them in the Council, and even to convict
their intentions of malice, made them foresee...

<meta>This page continues the biographical preface concerning the downfall of Boethius, following the mention of his virtuous life in the previous section.</meta>

->To the Reader<-

...a fatal outcome from their practices. All their care was therefore directed toward making his reputation suspect to their Master, and rendering his services useless to the Public. To this end, they forged letters from Boethius to the Emperor Justin <note>Justin I, Eastern Roman Emperor and defender of Catholic orthodoxy</note>, the sworn enemy of the Arians <note>Arianism was a Christian theological movement that denied the full divinity of Christ; it was the faith of the Ostrogothic rulers</note>, of whom Theodoric was the principal support in Italy. This plot was woven through the artifices of Cyprian, who had as witnesses to his calumny Opilio, Basilius, and Gaudentius. The King, without considering that envy always attaches itself to those who hold the primary confidence of the Prince, listened with too much faith to the suspicions they gave him regarding his <unclear>fidelity</unclear>, and instead of standing firm in the defense of such a noble Minister, he <unclear>very</unclear> basely abandoned him to the hatred of those who were jealous of him. Following the inclinations of Theodoric, the Senators—partly out of a desire to please, and partly out of a rivalry for greatness—condemned this great Man to banishment. Pavia had the good fortune of being the place of his prison, and the theater of his martyrdom. Theodoric having had the Governor of the City propose to him the confession of the conspiracy as an infallible means of his pardon, that courageous soul could not constrain himself to lie in order to live, choosing rather to suffer death than to love <unclear>shamefully</unclear>

<summary>This preface details the political conspiracy led by Cyprian to frame Boethius for treason against King Theodoric, resulting in his unjust imprisonment in Pavia. It highlights Boethius's integrity in refusing to falsely confess to a conspiracy even to save his own life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of Boethius during his imprisonment, specifically recounting his execution and the legendary circumstances surrounding his death, while introducing his most famous work.</meta>

->To the Reader<-

<sig>A 5</sig>

life. The Goth <note>Theodoric the Great, King of the Ostrogoths and ruler of Italy</note>, judging that Boethius was as little capable of feigning a treason as of committing one, dispatched a Tribune to execute his death sentence; no sooner was this served to him than he proceeded to the place of execution as if he were marching toward a triumph. When he noticed one of his gentlemen-in-waiting, who was dissolving into tears, he commanded him to save them for the wretched, and to tell Symmachus <note>Quintus Aurelius Memmius Symmachus, a Roman statesman and Boethius's father-in-law</note> his father-in-law, and Rusticiana his wife, not to do anything unworthy of him in their weeping, since he was doing nothing unworthy of them in his dying. After these noble words, it was not long before he lost his head, which he gathered up from the ground, like a second Saint Denis <note>The first Bishop of Paris, who, according to legend, carried his severed head after his execution (a "cephalophore")</note>, and carried it before the altar of a nearby chapel, where he knelt to offer it to that great God whose cause he had just defended. Martian <note>Likely referring to a medieval biographer or chronicler</note>, who described his life, asserts that when someone asked him, seeing him in this posture, who had caused his death, he replied that it was the impious. One may still see his prison in Pavia today. It was in this somber dwelling that he composed this precious work of *The Consolation* <note>*The Consolation of Philosophy*, written while Boethius awaited execution</note>, wherein he introduces Philosophy, who proposes to him all the reasons that might soften an affliction,

->To the Reader.<-

and which prepare a soul against the harshest attacks of <term>Fortune</term> <note>The personified Roman goddess of luck, often depicted with a wheel to symbolize the unpredictable rise and fall of human fates</note>. There is nothing rare or subtle found among the greatest Authors in which <term>Boethius</term> <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), the Roman statesman whose work bridged classical philosophy and medieval Christianity</note> does not also claim a share of that glory. His thoughts are sublime, his style polished, his reasoning profound, and his <term>Poetry</term> <note>The *Consolation* is written in 'prosimetrum,' a style that alternates between prose sections and lyrical poems</note> delicate. If the ear is charmed by the naturalness of his wit, the mind is persuaded by the weight of his arguments; if he sometimes softens his pen, he never weakens it; if he elevates his discourse, he never lets it wander; if he shines throughout, he always provides warmth; provided one pays attention, his work follows a logical flow; whoever brings eyes to the reading will find enlightenment.

For my part, I must confess that if this great <term>Philosopher</term> who worshipped the <term>Cross</term> had dedicated one of his chapters to it, and had represented for us a suffering <term>God</term> among these motives for <term>consolations</term>, then nothing would be lacking in his work, and despair would be reserved only for the <term>Reprobate</term> <note>The 'Réprouvez,' or those excluded from salvation</note>. Regardless of the cruelty with which <term>Fortune</term> persecutes us, we find here the means to cure our ills, or at least to soothe all our pains. If she robs us of our worldly goods, she teaches us to master ourselves; if she taints our pleasures with bitterness, she awakens us from the lethargy of an overly indulgent life; if she strips us of our

But what makes this piece even more worthy of recommendation is that the pleasant and the useful <note>A reference to the classical ideal of "prodesse et delectare"—to provide both profit and delight</note> are dispensed here with such judgment and skill that the sweetness of the <term>Poetry</term> <note>Boethius wrote in a style called "prosimetrum," which alternates between prose and verse</note> possesses no fewer attractions and innocent charms for the mind than the force of the discourse has power over the deepest wounds of the soul.

It is true that there is some obscurity in some of the Verses, because our <term>Poet-Philosopher</term> speaks sometimes like <term>Plato</term> <note>The Greek philosopher whose ideas on the soul's divine origin and the nature of the Good are central to the book</note>, and at other times he adapts himself to the approach of the <term>Stoics</term> <note>A school of philosophy that taught the endurance of pain or hardship without display of feelings and without complaint</note>. The diversity of these sentiments has sometimes excused me from the rigor that should restrict a <term>Translator</term> to the words of his Author, although I have endeavored to express almost all of his thoughts. If, to render a verse clearly, it seems that I occasionally employ <term>Paraphrase</term> <note>A restatement of a text to clarify its meaning, rather than a literal word-for-word translation</note>, I should be forgiven this infidelity, since I constrain myself with greater scrupulousness to all the Prose. I must admit, however, that another, who might be more of a slave [to the literal text] than I, would undoubtedly succeed better than I

than I have done, both in the one and the other. I can also protest that all the happiness and advantage I have in this will easily yield to the slightest effort of those who might wish to amuse themselves by translating <term>Boethius</term> <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose work, "The Consolation of Philosophy," was a cornerstone of medieval thought</note> for you. This undertaking well deserves the labor of a fine mind. Although the Greeks are modest enough in their esteem for Latin authors, <term>Maximus Planudes</term> <note>A 14th-century Byzantine scholar who famously translated Latin classics into Greek</note> translated this author into their language; and <term>Jean de Meun</term> <note>A prominent 13th-century French poet best known for "The Romance of the Rose"</note>, the first of our Frenchmen who strove not to be a "Barbarian" <note>In this context, "barbarian" refers to someone who lacks refinement in language or lacks knowledge of the classics</note>, judged in his own time that this version was not a gift unworthy of <term>Philip the Fair</term> <note>Philippe IV, King of France from 1285 to 1314</note>, his Master.

Nonetheless, if anyone wishes to judge what I have contributed to this piece, I beg them to consider that it is not easy to succeed when following the designs of another, particularly in verse, where one does not have the freedom to climb to Heaven, to descend into the abyss, or to stroll through meadows to pluck stars, flowers, and diamonds, which serve as the grace and beauty of <term>Poetry</term>. A man who is tied up can only stretch as far as the end of his rope; his power is no greater than his chain, and his liberty goes no further than the limits he is given. This leads me to believe that everything that is rough in this work,

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's justification from the previous page, where they compared the constraints of translation to being a man "tied up" who can only reach the end of his chain.</meta>

# To the Reader.

having such a reasonable excuse, the fear of too great a severity in my Reader would not be justified, and if I cannot merit his approval, I should not fear his censure much. I even wish to think fairly of those who will see this translation, and believe that there will be a good number who will approve that a person who is in the service of Philosophy acts as her interpreter at least once. If the great <term>Saint Thomas</term> <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas, a 13th-century theologian whose commentaries on Boethius were standard for students of the era</note> is praiseworthy for having left us a judicious Commentary on this *Consolation*, who could find it wrong that, to console everyone, I strive to make wisdom intelligible?

The entire piece is divided into five books: The first is only a complaint that the Author addresses to wisdom, regarding the evils that he has unjustly suffered. It is this great Lady whom he depicts in his first Prose, whose head touches the Heavens, inasmuch as she carries her knowledge there, and who afterwards adjusts herself to a reasonable height, because she lowers her thoughts to the consideration of inferior things. The <term>Theta</term> <note>original: "Thita"; referring to the Greek letter Θ, representing "Theoretike" or contemplative philosophy</note> and the <term>Pi</term> <note>The Greek letter Π, representing "Praktike" or practical philosophy</note>, which occupy the edges of her robe, signify that she encompasses Theory and Practice. By the blackness that coats her clothes, Boethius rebukes the ignorance of his age and the artifice of the first Philosophers. And to tell the truth, he has reason to suspect them of envy, or at least to reproach them for a lack of charity, since **Plato** disguises the sciences under Riddles, **Empedocles** renders them slaves in his verses, and **Aristotle** seems to speak of them to us only to be unintelligible.

The second [book] comprises an Apology for **Fortune**, which shows **Boethius** that he has as little reason to complain of his misfortunes as he has to hope for all her favors. The entire following part is nothing but an excellent weave of powerful considerations, which raise the spirit to this sublime truth: that neither Riches nor Dignities could <note>original: "ſçauroyent"; text partially obscured by ink blot</note> make a man content, and that we are never poor if we always belong to ourselves. The last Prose concludes this strange paradox: *that bad fortune benefits man more than good fortune.*

My READER, if you derive all the benefit that I wish for you from this Consolation, you may well be sometimes afflicted, but you will always be content <note>In the 17th-century sense, "content" implies a state of being fulfilled or at peace with one's lot, even amidst suffering.</note>.

## A P P R O B A T I O N <note>An "Approbation" was an official endorsement by a religious or academic authority, certifying that a book was both accurate and consistent with Church doctrine, often required before publication.</note>.

**THIS** *Book entitled, the Consolation of Philosophy, etc., approved through so many centuries, and admired by all those who have heard and practiced its doctrine, is translated so faithfully into our Language, that it gives us cause to believe there is nothing left in Verse or in Prose within the Treasures of antiquity that we cannot make our own; also, it deserves no less praise for its translation than it has been esteemed for the ra<unclear>re</unclear> virtues of its Author. This is why, besides the assurance I give that there is nothing in it that is not conforming to the Faith and Doctrine of the Catholic Church: It seems to me that it should be received as a masterpiece of the perfection of our language; which I, the undersigned Doctor of Theology and Chancellor of the University of Reims, certify by these presents. Done at Reims, this 3rd of March, 1636.*

->P. DOZET.<-

# A P P R O B A T I O N <note>An "Approbation" was an official endorsement by a religious or academic authority, certifying that a book was both accurate and consistent with Church doctrine, often required before publication.</note>.

**THIS** Book (which brings back to life that ancient Senator and Consul **BOETHIUS**,) does not so much need approval as it does praise; if in it Wisdom gives consolation to our spirit in adversity, the elevated style and elegant discourse with which it is enriched by this translation will give no less satisfaction in prosperity. I deem it most worthy of being published. This 2nd day of March, 1636.

->I. GODENOT, Doctor of Theology.<-

**I** do not object on behalf of the King—given that the term of the privilege <note>A "privilege" was a form of early copyright granted by the French crown, giving a specific printer the exclusive right to publish a work for a set number of years.</note> granted for the printing of the book entitled *The Consolations of Philosophy and Theology* by Father **DE CERIZIERS** has expired—to the said book being printed and published by Jean Huguetan the younger, Merchant Bookseller in this City, with the prohibitions <unclear>to</unclear> all others required in such cases. Done the twenty-second of August, one thousand six hundred and forty-seven.

#### POETRY I. <note>Boethius wrote this work in a style called "prosimetrum," which alternates between prose and verse. This first poem is an elegy, a traditional form for expressing grief and lamentation.</note>

*Though so many misfortunes lead with great strides,*

# I. PROSE.

As I was thus speaking to myself, and tracing my complaints with my pen, I seemed to see above my head a Lady full of majesty, whose eyes were much more lively and sparkling than those of ordinary men. Her complexion was fresh, and her cheeks had a fullness <note>original: "embon-point," meaning a healthy, robust appearance.</note> that had in no way declined, although her age made this beauty appear to belong to another era than our own. Her stature was not always consistent; for at one moment she shrank to a just and measured height, and then suddenly one would have thought she touched the stars. In lifting her head, she directed her gaze not only above the heavens <note>original: "les Astres," the stars or celestial bodies.</note>, but even human sight was too weak to follow her. Her garments were no less

...as much craftsmanship in their making as value in their fabric, especially since (as I learned from her own mouth) her own hands had woven them. Age had clouded them with a darkness quite like that of those ancient portraits from which men derive the brilliance of their nobility and the rays of their glory. <note>The author compares the darkened state of her clothes to old family portraits, suggesting that while Philosophy is timeless, she has been neglected or obscured over time.</note> At the bottom of her robe, one could see a <term>Pi</term> <note>The Greek letter Π (Pi), standing for "Praxis" or Practical life.</note> woven into the embroidery, and at the top a <term>Theta</term> <note>original: "Thita" (Θ), standing for "Theoria" or Contemplative life.</note>: between these two letters there were certain steps, forming a ladder from the lowest to the highest character. Yet it appeared from the tears in her robe that she had been pulled at with violence, and that each person had torn away whatever piece they could. <note>This allegorical detail suggests that various philosophical schools (like the Stoics or Epicureans) have each claimed a piece of the "truth" for themselves, tearing the unified garment of Philosophy.</note> This august Goddess held writing tablets in her right hand, and a scepter in her left. As soon as she perceived the Muses near me, and recognized that they were setting verses to the feelings of my grief, she was somewhat moved, and with a gaze mixed with severity, she cried out: "Who has permitted these brazen little creatures to approach this sick man? For it is not in their power to provide good or useful remedies for his ills, but only to nourish them with a sweet and pleasant poison. It is they who stifle the solid fruits of reason with the thorns of misdirected passions, and who accustom the mind to suffer ills from which they cannot deliver it. If your charms attracted an ordinary man, I would consider your audacity all the more pardonable, as his loss would be indifferent to me; but having addressed yourselves to a man raised in my schools, I cannot remain patient without being unfeeling. Get out of here, cursed Sirens, who flatter even unto death, and leave to me the care of his healing." This troop of Nymphs, lowering their eyes, withdrew very sadly, confessing their fear by their

# The Consolation

## II. PROSE.

"But it is time," she said, "to dress <note>original: "penser." In this period of French, "penser" was often used interchangeably with "panser," meaning to treat, bandage, or tend to a wound.</note> your wounds, and not to complain of them." Then, fixing her eyes upon me, she continued: "Are you the one who sucked the sweetness of my milk, who was nourished on my food, and who reached the age of a perfect man through my care? Certainly I had given you weapons that could have defended you, had you not abandoned them. Do you no longer know me? Whence comes this silence? Is it from shame or from stupor? I would very much like it to be from a reasonable sense of shame; but from what I see, it is from a dull stupor." When she perceived that my silence was rather an inability to speak than a choice to remain silent, and that I had as little tongue as I had words, she gently touched my chest and said: "Undoubtedly his illness is nothing other than this lethargy, <note>In the philosophical tradition of Boethius, "lethargy" is a state of spiritual amnesia where the soul forgets its divine origin and its inner strength due to worldly misfortune.</note> which is accustomed to dulling the Mind; he has slightly forgotten himself, but he can easily remember, if he can first recognize us. To help him, we must clear away these shadows that blind his eyes." When she had finished this speech, she gathered up the folds of her Robe, with which she wiped away my tears.

---

*Recalling the clarity,*
*And giving rays to the golden troop* <note>The "golden troop" refers to the stars, which appear to march across the sky and are revealed once the storm clouds have been swept away.</note>
*Which was hiding its beauty.*

### III. PROSE.

**T**he clouds of my sadness having vanished, I returned to the freedom of breathing, and I took the courage to look upon the face of my helpful Physician. Hardly had I cast my eyes upon this Goddess, than I recognized that good nurse in whose house I had spent the greater part of my youth—I mean to say, Philosophy—to whom I immediately spoke these words: "O holy Mistress of Virtues, why is it that you have left the delights of Heaven to join the solitudes of my exile? Are you not guilty of the same crimes that slander <note>This refers to the historical Boethius's own experience; he was a high-ranking official accused of treason and sorcery by political rivals, leading to his imprisonment and eventual execution.</note> has imposed upon me, that you should suffer these unjust penalties in my company?" "Is it reasonable," she replied, "my dear Nursling, <note>original: "Nourrisson." Philosophy views the narrator as her spiritual child, whom she has fed with the "milk" of wisdom.</note> to see you groaning under a burden with which the mere hatred of my name has charged you, without sharing this hardship with you? Wisdom <note>Philosophy uses "Wisdom" (Sagesse) here as a personification of her own nature and duty.</note> claims no right to leave innocence without support; I would fear being justly blamed if I abandoned you without cause. Do you believe that it is only today that Wisdom has been troubled by wicked morals? Do you not know that before the age of our

<summary>The narrator recovers from his stupor and recognizes Lady Philosophy as his lifelong mentor. Philosophy explains that she has come to his prison to share his burden, as Wisdom never abandons the innocent.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, recognition, exile, slander, Wisdom, nursling, spiritual healing</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Lady Philosophy. She is responding to the narrator’s (Boethius's) despair by reminding him that throughout history, those who follow her teachings have always faced persecution from the ignorant and the wicked.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>The Consolation</header>

Plato, I have endured harsh battles against the insolence of folly, and that through my means (while he was still alive) Socrates, his Master, won the honor of a glorious death? His memory has remained beyond reproach, but his heritage was not left without dispute, insofar as the School of the Stoics and that of Epicurus <note>Philosophy refers to how later philosophical sects tried to claim the authority of Socrates to justify their own conflicting doctrines.</note> have tried to claim him as their own; and although I offered resistance to their design, they pulled at me with such force that they tore my robe, which I had made, and withdrew with its shreds, believing that they would bring me over to their side if they adorned themselves with my spoils. This retreat somewhat favored their desire, for ignorance, seeing them dressed in my livery <term>livery: distinctive clothing worn by followers or servants; here, the outward appearance of being a philosopher</term>, believed they were my intimates, and by this appearance deceived some of the ignorant. 

But if you have not heard of the banishment of Anaxagoras, the poisoning of Socrates, and the tortures of Zeno <note>Anaxagoras was exiled from Athens; Socrates was famously forced to drink hemlock; Zeno of Elea was said to have been tortured for conspiring against a tyrant.</note>, because they are foreigners, surely you know of the Canii, the Senecas, and the Sorani <note>These are Roman martyrs of philosophy: Julius Canius was executed by Caligula; Seneca and Barea Soranus were forced to commit suicide by Nero.</note>, whose memory is not old, nor their glory unknown. Do not look for their excesses: their only crime was nothing other than the incompatibility my teachings gave them with the disposition of the Wicked. And therefore, it is without reason that you are astonished to see us tossed by some storm in this sea, since our principal aim is to displease the Scoundrels. And though their number be infinite, it is not for that more fearsome, because their enraged troop has no other guidance than fury. If at times malice gathers its forces against us, prudence puts us under the shelter of its insults, while malice busies itself taking up useless weapons; and thus entrenched in Forts, which are impenetrable to its assaults, we pay 

<meta>catchword: with mockery</meta>

<summary>Lady Philosophy explains that she and her followers have always been attacked by the wicked, using the metaphor of her torn robe to describe how different sects (Stoics and Epicureans) have distorted her true teachings.</summary>

### IV. POETRY.

*He who with the same eye looks upon Fortune,
Whether her passion flatters or troubles him
->In his prosperity;<-
That man, without turning pale, shall always keep his head
->Above the storm,<-
And his feet upon the brow of his adversity.*

*Though the foaming Sea stirs up its abyss,
Though it drenches the Heavens from the depths to the crest
->Of its proud waves,<-
And though Mount Gibel <note>original: "Mont-Gibel"; a historical name for Mount Etna in Sicily, derived from the Arabic 'Jabal' (mountain). It was a common literary symbol for uncontrollable destructive power.</note> vomits fire and flame,
->He enjoys in his soul<-
The happiness enjoyed by the spirit of the blessed.*

*The air may well hurl the bolts of its lightning,
And, changing rocks into heaps of dust,
->Display its power:<-
It may, with the voice of its mighty thunder,
->Make the Earth tremble,<-
But to make him feel fear is something that cannot be seen.*

*Let us have no desire, let us have no hope,
And we shall laugh at the pains caused by the violence
->Of proud tyrants:<-
But if we love and desire, we shall fear the anger
->Of the slightest misery;<-
And the smallest troubles shall be our Conquerors.*

*Whoever wishes to serve the inconstant Goddess <note>The "inconstant Goddess" is Fortune (Fortuna), traditionally depicted as a fickle woman with a wheel, representing the unpredictable ups and downs of life.</note>,*

## IV. PROSE.

Do these truths make any impression on your mind; or are you of the nature of that heavy animal <note>A reference to the "Ass with the Lyre," a classical proverb describing someone who is insensitive to art, music, or wisdom.</note>, which has neither ears nor taste for the pleasant sweetness of a lute? Why do you sigh? What is the subject of your tears? Wake yourself up a little, and do not allow sorrow to numb your spirit so. If you desire the healing of your wounds, you must reveal them.

Once I had rallied my thoughts a little, here is what I answered: Is the rigor of Fortune not pitiless enough toward me (my dear Mistress), that you judge it necessary to give her new instructions to be even more cruel to me? Could the horror of this prison not drive you away? Is this not that beautiful <term>library</term> <note>original: "Librairie"; in the 17th century, this referred to a personal study or collection of books where a scholar worked.</note> where you would sometimes come to discourse with me on human and divine sciences? Do I still have the same face and the same countenance I had when you marked out for me with a wand the course of the <term>stars</term> <note>In Boethius's time, the study of "stars" (astronomy) was a spiritual and mathematical pursuit intended to show the harmony of the universe.</note>, and when you related our movements and our inclinations to their influences, showing me that everything that happens within us is an image of what occurs in the Heavens? Is this the reward for the obedience I have rendered to your commands? Certainly, you had <term>Plato</term> <note>Boethius refers to Plato's *Republic*, specifically the famous "Philosopher King" argument.</note> say that Republics would be happy when philosophers were their gov-

Never did the consideration of any person make me bend toward injustice; I felt no less regret at seeing public taxes and private thefts than those who suffered the hardship.

# The Consolation

The hounds of the Court, who were already devouring him with greed and hope, and without fearing the hatred of **Cyprian** <note>Cyprianus, a high-ranking official and member of the King's council who sought to enrich himself by accusing others of treason.</note>, I protected **Albinus** <note>A Roman Senator whom Boethius defended against charges of treasonous correspondence with the Emperor in Constantinople.</note> from the punishment that a false accusation had brought upon him. Does it not seem to you that I have made enough enemies for myself? To tell the truth, having sought the favor of Courtiers so little for my own security, I ought to have found it among my friends; and since the love of Justice was the sole motive for my actions, it seems that Justice itself cannot be equitable without ensuring my preservation. 

Who are those whose testimonies have overpowered my innocence? Those very men who made use of the poverty of a wretched **Basilius**, who had been expelled from the King's household. What shall I say of **Opilio** and **Gaudentius**, whose excesses and injustices were punished by banishment, and who, having used the <term>privilege of the Altars</term> <note>original: "privilege des Autels"; the right of sanctuary in a church to avoid arrest.</note> against the King's command, were threatened—if they did not leave **Ravenna** <note>The capital city of the Ostrogothic Kingdom in Italy.</note> immediately—with having the marks of their disobedience branded upon their foreheads? Should any belief be given to such scoundrels? And yet, on that very same day, their deposition against me was accepted. 

What? Does my status make me a criminal? Or has their condemnation justified them? Has Fortune no shame for the calumny with which they have labored against my innocence, or at least for the baseness of those who have accused me? You ask me what crime has been imposed upon me? They say that I wished to save the **Senate**; you desire to know the means I had chosen to execute this design? They cry out that I prevented the Accuser from producing the proofs of its condemnation. 

What do you say to that, my holy Mistress? Do you wish for me to deny this fault, for fear that it might be shameful to you? But what? I did have this desire, and nothing in the world is capable of 

<meta>catchword: taking it away.</meta>

And then, what likelihood is there to think that the desire to save this flourishing **order** <note>The word "order" is struck through with a horizontal line in the original manuscript, likely reflecting a correction or a specific legal nuance regarding the Roman Senate.</note> was a sin? It is true that the poor counsel the Senate had taken regarding my life had justified the slanders of my enemies. Let it be so, I accept it; the imprudence of others will never change the obligation of my duty; if they are wicked, I shall not cease to be good. Even if I faced a **sentence** <note>original: "Arreſt"; referring to a final legal decree or judgment.</note> like that of **Socrates**, I would not believe it permitted for me to admit a lie or to conceal a truth. Whatever the case may be, I leave the judgment of this matter to your prudence and to that of all the Sages. Nevertheless, so that the memory of it might not be lost, I have left the pure truth of it in writing. As for those forged letters, in which I seemed to hope for Roman liberty, what need is there to speak of them? For it would have been easy to reveal the trickery, had I been permitted to use the testimony of the Accuser himself. And then, what liberty could one expect? Ah! would to God that such a hope could be reasonable! I would have used the noble response of **Canius** <note>Julius Canus, a Stoic philosopher sentenced to death by the Emperor Caligula; he is often cited as a model of composure and defiance against tyranny.</note>, who, when asked by **Caesar**, son of **Germanicus** <note>The Emperor Caligula.</note>, if he were an accomplice in the plot against his life, replied: "If I were, you would not have known it." In this, my displeasure has not so conquered my patience that I should complain of what the Wicked have undertaken against virtue. I only marvel that their evil design has had such success. Perhaps to desire lesser things would be a lack of courage; but to be able to achieve all that malice intends against innocence—that is a strange prodigy. It is there that one of your **nurslings** <note>original: "Nourriſſons"; a term for the disciples or "foster-children" of Lady Philosophy who have been raised on her teachings.</note> found cause to slightly...

But it is enough to be guilty simply to be your friend; I am certain that my entire crime comes from the inclination I have had to receive your instructions. And thus it was not enough that your esteem was useless to me, if my misfortune were not also a reproach to you. What makes the height of my misery is that the sentiment of most men regards the outcomes of **Fortune** <note>The personified goddess of chance and luck, who Boethius famously argues is fickle and unreliable.</note> rather than the merit of things, and judges only those things to be wisely planned which have a happy success; from which it happens that a good reputation is never found alongside bad fortune. I would never finish if I wished to recount here the diversity of thoughts and opinions; I will only tell you that the unfortunate are always considered guilty, and that they are deemed worthy of the punishments they cannot avoid. And I, who am stripped of my riches, deprived of my offices, and soiled in my reputation, have merited punishments for...

Give her alone the advantage, <note>This continues the description of the Moon from the previous page; her brightness makes her superior to the stars.</note>
Over all the stars of sleep.

It is by your wise guidance,
That the evening in the Firmament
Gives the stars their motion,
And the morning puts them to flight:
Without you the rigor of winters
Would not take away from the green trees,
That which makes them so agreeable:
The flowers would keep their color,
By the care of favorable winds,
Which cool the heat.

What the breath of Boreas, <note>original: *Borée*. In Greek mythology, Boreas is the purple-winged god of the cold north wind, bringing winter.</note>
Has caused beauty to perish,
Is seen again when the Summer
Brings back the Empire of Rhea: <note>original: *l'Empire de Rhée*. Rhea was the mother of the Olympian gods, often identified with Cybele and the fertile Earth itself.</note>
The same gain that the icicles
Seemed to steal from the harvests,
Falls finally beneath the sickle,
And the diligent Laborer,
Uses the hands of his family,
To gather all his happiness.

There is no creature
Who does not know its duty,
And who does not follow the will
Of the great Author of Nature;
Man alone, the masterpiece of the Heavens,
Like a most odious object,
Is withdrawn from your providences,
And from the effects of your support,
Although the end of his sufferings
Can only come from you. <note>In the original French, "luy" (him) refers back to God (the Author of Nature). Boethius is questioning why God, who controls the stars and seasons so perfectly, seems to leave human affairs to chance or injustice.</note>

On Philosophy. Book I. 37

## V. PROSE.

**M**y grief having thus broken out, and my impatience having unburdened my heart of these few sighs, Wisdom <note>original: *la Sagesse*. Referring to Lady Philosophy, the personification of Wisdom.</note> looks at me with a smiling face; and without being much moved by my complaints, she says to me: When I saw you sad and weeping, I immediately knew that you were wretched and banished; but if your speech had not helped me, I would still be wondering how far the place of your exile is from your homeland, though I consider you rather a little separated from it than banished. That you believe yourself driven out of it—it is your own error that creates this exile rather than the truth, since no one has ever been able to have such power over you. If you remember your homeland, you will know that it is not governed by the People, as that of the Athenians is, but that there is only one Master and one King, who draws great pleasure from the vast number of his Citizens, and who makes his subjects free through the service they render to him. <note>Philosophy contrasts the democracy of Athens with the divine "monarchy" of the soul's true home, where serving God is the highest form of freedom.</note> Have you forgotten this maxim, which holds that all those who have settled their desires in this place of delights cannot be banished from it, since exile is not to be feared by those who have no wish other than for Heaven, and he who ceases to desire this dwelling, ceases to deserve it? This is why I am not as astonished by the horror of this place as by that of your face; and I do not seek the polished marbles and shining windows of your study so much as the strength of that mind, in which I once placed all the rarest treasures of my knowledge <note>original: *sciences*. In this context, it refers to the various branches of learning and philosophical truths.</note>. Regarding your good deeds, the feeling you have for them is true, but it does not yet equal their...

<sig>C 3</sig>

<vocab>Philosophy, Wisdom, exile, Athenians, Master, King, Heaven, treasures, good deeds</vocab>

<summary>Lady Philosophy begins her response to Boethius's lament, telling him that his true "exile" is not his physical imprisonment, but his loss of memory regarding his spiritual homeland and its divine Ruler. She emphasizes that a mind centered on God cannot be forcibly banished from its true home.</summary>

# VI. PROSE

**W**ill you allow me to probe the dispositions of your Soul by asking a few questions: which I shall do only so that I may recognize the means I must use for your healing. If you desire to clarify any doubts, you may interrogate me with freedom. In the first place, do you believe that the conduct of the world is an effect of Fortune, or of reason? "Truly," (I replied) "I shall never hold the opinion that things so certain and so regulated are guided by uncertainty; on the contrary, I believe that God takes care of His work, and I am certain that nothing in the World could divert me from this belief." "You are right," (replied Philosophy) "yet I remember that you were complaining just now that only man, abandoned by his pro-" <note>The text cuts off here, likely leading into "protection" or "providence."</note>

B. To tell the truth, I have difficulty conceiving your question, let alone being able to provide a good answer.

# Of Philosophy. Book I.

...driven from your home: and because you are ignorant of the end <note>The word "end" here refers to the *telos* or ultimate purpose of human existence.</note> of man, the Wicked appear happy to you, provided they are powerful. Having forgotten the governance of Beings, you believed that everything happened by chance; all these faults cause not only evil, but they even bring death. I give thanks, nonetheless, to your Preserver <note>Referring to God as the protector of the soul's spark.</note> that He has not let you perish entirely. I have a remedy that will return you to a state of perfect health: it is the firm faith in a Providence, which you say conducts itself by reason, and not by the blindness of fate <note>The original OCR reads "fort," but in this period’s typography, it is a transcription of "sort" (fate/chance).</note>. Fear nothing; from this little spark, you begin to receive a healing warmth. But since this is not the season to use stronger and more violent remedies, and because our spirit embraces false opinions after having neglected the true ones—from which it <unclear>happens</unclear> that reason cannot see the brilliance of truth—I wish to treat it with a gentler regime. Thus, having dissipated the shadows of your error, you may turn your eyes toward the clear lights of truth, and not toward the appearance of a lie.

<meta>This page continues the poetic interlude from the end of Book I (Metrum 7). Lady Philosophy uses metaphors of nature—the moon obscured by clouds, muddied water, and winding streams—to explain how human emotions cloud the clarity of reason and prevent the soul from seeing the truth.</meta>

# Consolation
<meta>catchword: BOOK</meta>

The Moon in the clouds
Is hidden from our eyes,
And shows itself clearly
Only to the Stars of the Heavens.

When we see the silt <note>The original French uses *areine* (sand or sediment). In this context, it refers to how stirred-up debris makes water opaque.</note>
Washing atop the water,
We would be hard-pressed
To see our reflection there.

The proud torrent,
That runs while winding,
Drags itself over the grass
Like a Serpent.

Nevertheless, its course
Seems to seek out
The opening of its source
When it strikes a rock <note>This suggests that even a diverted stream tries to return to its origin, much like the soul seeks its divine source despite obstacles.</note>.

Do you want your joys
To be without change,
And all your ways
Without straying?

Banish the hope
For a deceptive object;
Let confidence
Steady your fear.

Our soul sighs,
When its passions
Usurp the rule
Over our actions.

<note>This concludes the first book. Philosophy argues that emotions like joy, fear, hope, and grief are "clouds" that obstruct the mind's natural light.</note>

## I. PROSE.

<image-desc>Large ornamental woodcut initial 'A' decorated with intertwined vines and leaves.</image-desc>After having spoken thus, she remained silent for some time; and then, having made me attentive to her discourse by the gentle gravity of her silence, she continued in this way. If I am not mistaken in my knowledge of the causes and the state of your illness: it is the desire for your former fortune <term>Fortune: Often personified as the goddess Fortuna, representing the fickle and unpredictable nature of luck, wealth, and worldly status.</term> that afflicts you: it is its change alone that has altered the good disposition of your soul. I begin to perceive the artifices of that traitress <note>Lady Philosophy refers to Fortune here as a deceptive woman who lures men into a false sense of security.</note>, who feigns a close friendship in order to more easily deceive those she wishes to destroy, and to burden them with true sorrows through her disguised caresses. If you remember her nature, and if you have not forgotten her worth, you will not believe you possessed anything of consequence in her favor, nor lost anything precious by her disgrace. It will not be difficult to recall to your mind the memory of these things; since you used to reject her flatteries with noble disdain, and criticize her fickleness with maxims <note>original: "sentences." <meta>This page continues the speech of Lady Philosophy from the end of the previous chapter. The Consolation

<meta>catchword: That</meta>

...pleasant medicine, to prepare you for stronger and more violent remedies. Let this Eloquence, which always has good effects when it follows my instructions, speak to you a little, and let Music <note>Lady Philosophy uses music and rhetoric as "sweeteners" to make her difficult truths more palatable to the suffering Boethius.</note>, which is but the least of my servants, mix with it the charming chords of its harmony. What is it that troubles you, poor man? Perhaps the experience of your misfortunes has shown you something new in the world? If you believe that Fortune <term>Fortune: The personification of luck and fate, often depicted as a woman turning a wheel.</term> has changed toward you, you are mistaken: this is her usual way, this is her nature; if she has overturned your prosperity, she has been constant in her character. She is the same one who formerly flattered you with a vain hope of felicity <term>felicity: A state of intense happiness or high-ranking social success.</term>. You have seen the full face of this blind Divinity <note>original: "aueugle Diuinité." A reference to Fortuna being blind or blindfolded, showing she does not choose her favorites based on merit.</note>; she who remains hidden from others is now perfectly known to you. Do you understand her custom? Make use of this knowledge; do not complain about it. If you fear her betrayal, despise her caresses, all the more since she who is now the subject of your displeasure was just recently the cause of your peace. She who has abandoned you is the same one from whom no one can promise to be constantly followed. Perhaps a happiness that must soon depart seems significant to you, and you value this precious Fortune, whose enjoyment is uncertain and whose loss is lamentable. For if it is impossible to retain her at our will, and if she creates miserable people when she departs, her fickleness is an infallible sign of future misery. It is not enough to stop at the present; prudence <note>The cardinal virtue of looking ahead and making wise choices based on future consequences.</note> looks to the <unclear>future</unclear>, and thus ensures that one does not desire the favor of her caresses very much. Furthermore, since you submitted your desires to the will of Fortune, you have imposed upon yourself a law to accept all her actions.

<summary>Lady Philosophy begins her treatment of Boethius by explaining the fickle nature of Fortune, arguing that her very inconsistency is the only thing a person can truly rely on. She urges him to use his reason to see that losing worldly status is simply a part of Fortune's natural cycle.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Lady Philosophy’s personification of Fortune. She argues that Boethius, by engaging with Fortune, has effectively entered into a contract with an inherently unstable force and has no right to complain when her nature—changeability—asserts itself.</meta>

### of Philosophy. Book II.

But if you wish for her to come, and to stay whenever it seems good to you, are you not making a servant of her whom you chose as your mistress, and increasing your misery through your anxiety? If you were sailing upon the Sea, the winds would carry you, and not the movements of your desire; if you sowed the fields, the fertility of one year would soften the sterility of the other. You have given yourself over to Fortune <term>Fortune: Often personified as a fickle goddess, "Fortuna," who controls the earthly fates of men without regard for merit.</term>, and so it is for you to follow her lead, and not for her to study your inclinations. What madness is this? You wish to stop the wheel of Fortune; if she begins to be constant, she ceases to be Fortune.

## II. PROSE.

I would like to say a few words <note>original: "trois mots" — literally "three words," a common French idiom for "a few words."</note> in her favor; judge for yourself if her request is fair. Why do your complaints accuse me every day as if I were a criminal? What outrage have I committed against you? What goods have I taken from you? I am willing to dispute the enjoyment of riches and honors before an Arbiter of your choice, and if any of these things belong to men, I will frankly admit that it is an act of violence to snatch from you what you reclaim with so many sighs. 

When nature brought you forth from your mother’s womb, I received you quite naked in my arms; since then, I have supported you with my goods, and—what bothers you now—I raised you with too much kindness <note>original: "courtoisie" — implying the refined treatment of a noble court.</note> by giving you almost all the right that I have to riches. If it pleases me now to withdraw them, thank me for the use I permitted you to have of them, and do not murmur at the loss you suffer, since it was only a loan <term>loan: A central Stoic and Boethian concept: worldly goods are "borrowed" from Fortune and must eventually be returned.</term> and not a gift. 

You would have reason to complain and your regrets would be reasonable if you were losing something that was your own. Why do you sigh? I have done you no wrong; riches, honor, and greatness belong to my domain; they are my servants. When I go somewhere, they follow me; if I leave, they accompany me. I dare to say with certainty that if these goods, whose loss you deplore, had been yours, you would possess them still. Shall I be the only one who cannot exercise my rights? One does not get angry that

<meta>catchword: the Heavens</meta>

<summary>Lady Philosophy, speaking as Fortune, argues that wealth and status are merely "loans" to humanity rather than permanent possessions. She asserts her right to take back what she gave, as these things are part of her domain and not the inherent property of Boethius.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the speech of Fortune (personified by Lady Philosophy). Having argued that Boethius never truly owned his wealth, she now uses the changing of seasons and historical examples to prove that her nature is inherently unstable and that he should have expected his downfall.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>On Philosophy. Book II.</header>

The Heavens hide their most beautiful days in a very dark night. The year has the freedom to crown the earth with flowers, to burden it with fruit, to sow it with roses like pearls, and then to freeze it with cold and frost. The whiteness of the snows with which the year covers the earth shows nothing but its innocence. One is indeed astonished to see the sea when storms stir it up, but one does not complain about it more than when its waves are smooth and calm. And shall men, to satisfy an insatiable desire for wealth, wish to constrain me to constancy, which is entirely contrary to my nature? 

Here is my game: I turn a wheel ceaselessly; I take pleasure in raising low things and lowering high ones. Climb if you wish, but on the condition that you will not feel offended to descend when chance brings it about. Were you ignorant of my custom? Did you not know that Croesus, <note>Croesus: The King of Lydia legendary for his immense wealth.</note> King of the Lydians, was a deplorable subject of compassion to Cyrus—to whom he had caused so much fear—and that he was only protected from the flames of his pyre by a rain that fell fortuitously from the Heavens? 

Have you forgotten that Paul <note>Paul: Referring to Lucius Aemilius Paullus Macedonicus (c. 229–160 BCE), a Roman general who wept for the captured King Perseus of Macedon, recognizing that the same fate could eventually befall Rome.</note> mingled his tears with those of Perseus, his captive, and that he could not be happy in the midst of a triumph? The theaters sing of nothing else but the blows of Fortune, who, without any discretion, overturns the happiness of kingdoms and provinces. 

Did you not learn as a young child that there are two jars <note>original: "vaisseaux" — literally vessels. This is a reference to Homer's *Iliad*, where Jupiter/Zeus has two urns, one of blessings and one of sorrows, from which he pours out the fates of men.</note> beside Jupiter, one of which is full of blessings and the other of evils? What will you say if I show you that I have given you more of the former than you have known of the latter? What? If I have not entirely moved away from you? What? If my instability is a just cause for hope for you? Nevertheless, for fear that your spirit might be too greatly afflicted, and that...

## II. POETRY.

When Fortune with full hands,
Were to pour out upon humans
As many goods as the Sky has Stars,
When the night covers us with its veils,
And when the Moon upon her return
Tries to create a second day;
When the Ocean had no more sands <note>A poetic way of saying "as many grains of gold as there are grains of sand in the sea."</note>,
They would still believe themselves miserable.
Though God, lavish with his gold,
Exhausted all his treasure for them:
Though his goodness, to win victory
Over their desires, presented them with his glory;
Their invincible ambition
Will remain without satisfaction,
The burning thirst of this greed <term>conuoitiſe: a strong desire for worldly possessions, often translated as "cupidity."</term>,
The more it drinks, the more it is kindled,
One never possesses anything
If one believes oneself to have no wealth.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the narrator (Boethius) and Lady Philosophy. On the previous page, Philosophy—speaking as Fortune—argued that change is an inherent law of nature. This page transitions from the "gentle" comforts of rhetoric to a more rigorous philosophical examination of the narrator's life.</meta>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>On Philosophy. Book II.</header>

...quence <note>The word is truncated from the previous page: "eloquence."</note>, and to Music: but they only flatter the skin for as long as they touch the ear. The feelings of a miserable person are much deeper, from which it happens that pain begins to make us suffer again when these beautiful words cease to tickle us. "I admit it," replied *Philosophy*, "because I do not yet bring the true remedies for your ills, but only apply a lenitive <term>lenitive: a soothing medicine or treatment that eases pain or tension without curing the underlying cause.</term> to your impatience. When the time comes, I will prepare some that will reach the very bottom of the wound. Nevertheless, so that you contribute nothing to the exaggeration of your own misfortune, do you not remember your past prosperities? I leave aside the fact that after the death of your father, the care of the most honorable men of the City <note>A reference to Rome; after his father's death, Boethius was raised by aristocratic mentors like Symmachus.</note> was directed toward your protection; you were pleasing to them before being allied to them, which is a more noble way of belonging than that of blood. Who would not judge you happy to have found a father-in-law of such rare merit? <note>Boethius married Rusticiana, the daughter of Symmachus, one of the most respected senators of the age.</note> A wife of such perfect honesty, and with all these advantages, to possess a son? I intentionally overlook common favors; I could say that your youth was honored with the same offices that had been refused to old men. I wish to come to the pinnacle of your greatness. If there is anything significant among the things here below, should the feeling of the most extreme miseries erase from your memory that glorious day in which you saw your two sons, amid the applause of the Senate and the praises of the people, declared Consuls <term>Consul: the highest political office in the Roman state; Boethius's sons were co-consuls in 522 AD.</term>, and when you earned, by an excellent speech, the reputation of a great mind and a perfect orator? Can you not remember the day on which, seated between your two Consuls in the Circus, you represented to the Romans the glory and magnificence of ancient triumphs?"

<meta>catchword: If</meta>

If I am not mistaken, you used to pay fine compliments to Fortune when she caressed you like her dearest delight; certainly you have received a benefit that no one else had ever obtained from her goodwill. Do you then wish to settle accounts with her? This is the first time she looks upon you a little less favorably. If you consider your prosperity and your misfortunes, you still could not deny that you are happy. If you judge the opposite because you no longer possess the things you once held, you have no reason to believe yourself miserable, since the evils that afflict you now will soon pass away. Perhaps you have only just come into the world <note>Philosophy suggests Boethius is acting with the naivety of a child who does not understand that life is inherently changeful.</note>. The inconstancy of her conduct disturbs you, even though a single moment ruins man himself, who is the most noble part of it. Although there is no certainty in things governed by fate, the last day of our life is nonetheless the certain death of Fortune. What does it matter whether you leave her by dying, or whether she abandons you by fleeing?

---

...a bad life; I will not have much difficulty in granting it, provided that you do not cast the blame for it upon the nature of things. If the name of a fleeting happiness flatters you, I wish to show you with how many true goods you are still rich. For if Fortune has taken away her lesser comforts, yet left you the most significant ones, do you not have more reason to praise her courtesy than cause to accuse her of misfortunes? The glory of all mankind lives on, if your father-in-law is not dead. <note>Philosophy refers to Quintus Aurelius Memmius Symmachus, a highly respected Roman statesman and Boethius's father-in-law, who was known for his wisdom and integrity.</note> And that which you undoubtedly prize more than your own life—that great man who is composed only of virtues and wisdom—having no injuries of his own to complain of, sighs for yours instead. The rarest example of modesty and chastity lives in your Wife, whose every praise can be gathered into this single word: that she resembles her father, Symmachus. She lives, but she lives for you alone, through the ardent desire she has to see you again; in which, to hide nothing, I will frankly admit that you are a little less happy, since the condition of your present life and the knowledge of your weakness cause her to waste away with grief. <note>original: "la font mourir" - literally "make her die," used here metaphorically to describe her intense sorrow.</note> What shall I say of your two Sons, in whom, children though they are, I notice the courage of their father and their grandfather? Oh, how happy you are to still possess even now what everyone believes to be more precious than life! Wipe away these tears then; Fortune is not yet your enemy to the ultimate degree, and this storm that tosses you is not dangerous, since the anchor that holds you provides relief for the present and gives you hope for the future.

**B.** May that remain with me, and let the rest go as it may; I shall try to pull myself from this shipwreck. Yet you see what I have lost.

**P.** We have already advanced somewhat, if your condition is not entirely <unclear>unbearable</unclear>;

## IV. POETRY.

*Whoever wants to enjoy a secure peace,*

*The lowest seat is the most immovable:*
*Stop your skiff at the humblest rock,*
*If you want the wind not to be able to touch you:*
*For even though it shakes the Heaven with its storm,*
*You will see it, without fear, pass over your head.*

## V. PROSE.

But since my reasons are beginning to advance your recovery, I judge it appropriate to add more powerful ones. Pray tell me, even assuming that the goods of <term>Fortune</term> <note>Philosophy personifies "Fortune" as a fickle goddess who grants and withdraws worldly success on a whim.</note> were not subject to <term>vicissitude</term> <note>original: "vicissitude" — the constant changing or shifting of circumstances.</note>, do they have anything capable of exciting desire in you, and which is not worthy of your contempt? <meta>Continuing from page 55, where Philosophy began to question the intrinsic value of Fortune's gifts. This page continues the prose section, specifically examining why material objects like money and jewels are unworthy of a rational soul's devotion.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>The Consolation</header>

...precious by their own nature, or by the opinion that you conceive of them? Is gold the most significant part of it, or rather heaps of silver? Truth be told, they shine more vividly in the hand of a **spendthrift**, than in the purse of a **miser**, since profusion always has more luster than stinginess. For if a gift no longer remains in the power of the one who gives it, money only begins to be useful when one begins to no longer possess it. If all the riches in the world were gathered to make a single man rich, they would render all other men miserable. In this, riches are contrary to the **voice**, which allows itself to be possessed by everyone <note>The author compares sound to money: many people can hear the same word and "possess" it fully at once, whereas money must be divided and lost to be shared.</note> without being divided among anyone. And thus, when wealth leaves a house, that house remains poor. Alas! How little are riches to be desired, since they can only be half-possessed, and cannot make a single man powerful except through the poverty of many others. 

Do your eyes not allow themselves to be caught by the brilliance of **precious stones**? If they have some ray of light, it belongs to the **Diamonds**, and not to man; and thus I admire your admiration, when it has no other subject than the beauty of stones. Is there anything among these inanimate bodies—where one finds no distinction of parts, or which are without movement—that can rightfully please a **rational creature**? <note>Philosophy argues that since humans have reason and life, it is beneath them to find "happiness" in unthinking, unmoving objects like rocks.</note> 

Even if all these things are beautiful by the <term>artifice</term> <note>original: "artifice" — here meaning the divine craftsmanship or skill of the Creator.</note> of Him who made them, when compared to your own beauty, they are beautiful no longer; and therefore I see nothing that deserves your ecstasies except for your too-great readiness to value them. Does the <term>enamel</term> <note>original: "Esmail" — a poetic term describing the vibrant, jewel-like colors of a flowering field.</note> of the fields flatter your senses? Why not? It is a beautiful half of an excellent work. In the same way, the surface of the Sea pleases us when the winds do not place ripples upon it; thus the Sky, and the Stars offer

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 56, where Lady Philosophy argued that jewels and gold have no intrinsic value to a rational human soul. This page continues her discourse, shifting focus from inanimate minerals to the beauties of nature and the vanity of social status.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>of Philosophy. Book II.</header>

...offer a thousand ravishing attractions to our eyes. Does any of these beauties belong to you? Would you dare take credit for their merit? Do the flowers of Spring adorn you? Do the fruits of Autumn come from your own fertility? Why do you take such vain complacency in them? And what good is it to attribute to yourself what belongs to another? Fortune can never give you what the condition of nature has made uniquely theirs. The fruits of the Earth are owed for the nourishment of animals: if you simply wish to satisfy your natural desire, I do not see that it is necessary to seek the superfluities of Fortune, especially since nature is content with little, and everything that is superfluous either harms or annoys <note>Philosophy echoes the Stoic and Epicurean idea that "natural" needs are easily met, while "artificial" desires created by Fortune are endless and burdensome.</note>. 

Perhaps you draw great advantage from the pomp of clothes; yet their material comes from nature, and their fashioning from the Tailor. Does a long train of servants make you happy? If they are wicked, you drag along with you the apprehension of a gang of thieves, rather than the convenience of a great number of valets; if they are good, does their goodness increase your own? From which I conclude that of all that you attribute to yourself, there is nothing that properly belongs to you. If you confess that these things have nothing excellent in them, why will you grieve at their loss, or why will you rejoice in their possession? 

For if all these things are beautiful by their own nature, you should value them just as much when they are not in your power as if they were your own; they are not precious or good simply because they are among your possessions, or because you have esteemed them as such. What do you seek with so much pain and care? Perhaps to chase away poverty through abundance? The exact opposite happens to you, since it requires much help and assistance to preserve many goods.

<sig>D 5</sig>

<summary>Lady Philosophy argues that external goods like nature’s beauty, fine clothing, and many servants are not part of a person's true self. She concludes that wealth does not cure poverty but rather creates a new dependency on others to protect one's many possessions.</summary>

And so it is true that the Great have great needs, and that the small, who rule their desires by necessity and not by the excess of ambition, can do without almost everything. But what? Have you nothing of your own, that you must have recourse to <term>external goods<gloss>In Stoic and Platonic philosophy, these are "indifferents" like wealth or fame that exist outside the soul.</gloss></term>; is the good state of things so changed that this creature, whom reason places among the divine things, believes himself to be without luster if he does not shine with the brilliance of another? 

The Beings are content with what they have, yet man, whose spirit is a Divinity <note>Philosophy refers to the "divine spark" of human reason, which aligns the soul with the Creator.</note>, seeks (to the shame of his Creator) the ornaments of the vilest creatures. He has raised the excellence of men above all other Beings, and you lower that dignity below the most wretched things. 

In this, certainly, you do yourself a signal injury; for if the good is always better than that which it makes good, by placing your happiness in the enjoyment of lowly things, by your own admission, you recognize yourself as even lesser than they; inasmuch as the nature of man is of such a condition that she surpasses all things when she knows herself, and becomes inferior to them when she forgets her dignity. 

It is a natural disadvantage for Animals to be ignorant of themselves, but in man it is a vice—and a vice that goes quite deep, since you judge that external perfections can be advantageous to you, though it be impossible; for the luster of that which shines cannot give merit to that which has none. 

For my part, I could never grant that what harms its possessor is good. Perhaps I am mistaken; yet I know that you do not contradict me. Riches, therefore, are but false goods, since they have caused true evils to those who have possessed them: since the most wicked have judged themselves the most worthy to have all that is
<meta>catchword: of gold, and</meta>

<summary>Lady Philosophy argues that human dignity is derived from self-knowledge and reason, and that seeking value in external wealth is a "vice" that makes humans inferior to the very objects they possess. She concludes that riches are false goods because they often bring real harm to their owners.</summary>

## VI. PROSE.

What shall I say of the honors and the greatness that your ignorance raises even to the Heavens? What eruption of Mount Gibel <note>Mount Gibel is an archaic name for Mount Etna, derived from the Arabic *Jebel* (mountain).</note>, what Deluge will cause as many evils as a wicked man who holds power? If you have not lost your memory, you may remember that The Proud [City] <note>Refers to Rome's history; "La Superbe" likely personifies the Roman state or its pride in expelling the kings.</note>, which had chased the Kings from the Empire, [later] rejected the government of the Consuls <term>Consuls<gloss>The two highest-ranking officials in the Roman Republic, elected annually to lead the state.</gloss></term>, even though it had been the beginning of its liberty. If sometimes honors are bestowed upon people of merit, nothing pleases us there but the integrity of those who use them well; and thus it happens that Virtue honors the office, and not the office Virtue. 

And I ask you, what is this power for which you have so many desires? Do you not take notice, men of clay <note>original: "hommes de bouë" (men of mud/clay), a humbling reminder of human mortality and earthly origins.</note>, do you not take notice of whom you command? Could you keep from laughing if you saw a rat acting the King among other rats, and usurping the Empire of that powerful people? Is there anything weaker than the body of man, from whom the sting of a gnat can take away life? And nonetheless, all the power of Monarchs extends no further, since it can do nothing except <unclear>to</unclear> the <unclear>body,</unclear>

A **tyrant** having put a **Philosopher** to the torture, in order to extract a confession of the accomplices of a conspiracy that had been plotted against his life, this great courage bit off his tongue and spat it in the face of his executioner; and thus the torments that the **Tyrant** considered a matter of cruelty, our **Sage** made into a matter of virtue <note>This story is frequently attributed to the philosopher Zeno of Elea or Anaxarchus, who supposedly bit off their tongues to avoid betraying their comrades under torture.</note>. Can one do any evil that one might not suffer from another? **Hercules** made **Busiris** <note>Busiris was a mythical King of Egypt who sacrificed all foreigners who entered his country; Hercules eventually killed him on his own altar.</note> submit to the same laws he himself had made. **Regulus** <note>Marcus Atilius Regulus, a Roman general of the Third Century BCE who was eventually captured and tortured by the Carthaginians after previously taking many of them prisoner.</note>, putting several **Carthaginians** in chains, learned how one ought to be bound. Will you then judge a person to be powerful if they can only do the evil that they themselves can suffer? Furthermore, if **dignities** had any natural goodness, they would never have any commerce with the wicked, since there is an impossibility between contrary things. And therefore it must be accepted as truth that honors have nothing good in them, since they allow themselves to be possessed by **Scoundrels**. The most beautiful presents of **Fortune** are ordinarily the rewards of vice. I will add further that no one doubts that he is strong who has strength, and he is light who has agility: in the same way **Music** makes **Musicians**, **Medicine** makes **Doctors**, and **Rhetoric** makes **Orators**, because each thing gives the effect that is natural to it, and drives away that which is contrary to it. **Riches** do not quench the thirst of **avarice**, nor does power submit itself to one who obeys vice. Thus dignity reveals those who are unworthy of it, rather than making them capable of it. From where does it come, then, that men call them so, if it is not that you take pleasure in giving this name of **good**
