*Wished to be the judge of the charms now passed,*
*Which had just expired in her frozen limbs.*
*This brute, nonetheless, limited his world*

*Bring Nero back to the bounds of duty?*
*Pitiless fate, when art and artifice*
*Aid, with impunity, the license of vice.*

To make you understand the vanity of this design, I pray you to recall to your memory that the entire Earth, compared to the Heavens, has almost no extent, as **Astronomy** <note>original: "Astrologie." In the 17th century, the terms for astronomy and astrology were often used interchangeably to describe the study of celestial bodies.</note> has taught you; and of this little World, if we believe **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), an Egyptian-Greek mathematician and astronomer whose geocentric model of the universe was standard for centuries.</note>, barely a fourth part is inhabited by men and animals. If we now consider in this part how much the Seas and Lakes drown, and how much the Solitudes and Deserts occupy, men have almost no place left to dwell. What blindness! You wish to extend your glory within this narrow strait, and dilate your reputation within the point of a point. <note>A mathematical metaphor emphasizing that the Earth is a mere dot in the vastness of the cosmos.</note> But what greatness can the glory of men have in so small a space, if it is not the disorder of ambition? Add to this consideration that this little bit of earth is shared among an infinity of Peoples, who are no less separated by customs than by the distance of the lands that separate them; and thus there is no fame strong enough—not even that of entire Cities—that can cross so many Seas and so many Mountains. In the time of **Cicero**, Roman glory did not extend beyond the **Caucasus**, although it was then in its greatest brilliance, and the **Parthians** <note>The Parthian Empire was a major political and cultural power in ancient Iran and a long-term rival to Rome.</note> trembled with fear at its power. ### The Consolation

[Where can] Rome go, where the glory of its Empire has not been able to penetrate? And then, are you unaware that actions which are worthy of praise among one Nation merit punishment among another People, so poorly do they agree on the opinion of virtue? From which it is easy to infer that a man who is in love with his reputation should not desire to extend it to many Nations. Consequently, he who is content with the good esteem he possesses in his own country will find the whole immensity of his glory bounded within a single Province.

How many fine examples has forgetfulness lost for lack of Writers? But of what use is History itself, since time <note>original: "l'âge." In this context, it refers to the passage of long eras or antiquity.</note> consumes both Books and Authors? And do you think to give immortality to your name if you cause it to pass through thought to the centuries to come? What reason will you have to be vain, if you compare the duration of your reputation to that of eternity? A moment has some proportion, however small, with ten thousand years, because the duration of both is finite; but certainly, however great the duration of your glory may be, it will never reach that eternity, since the former suffers boundaries and the latter has none. And from this it follows that a reputation of many centuries, compared to this immensity of time, is not small—rather, it is nothing at all. 

Strange thing! That you learn to do good out of the vanity of a little noise <note>A metaphor for "fame" or "reputation," which Lady Philosophy dismisses as mere sound in the air.</note>, and not from the true glory of virtue, nor from the testimony of your conscience! Listen to how pleasantly a certain man mocks this weakness. Someone having attacked with insults a man who wished to appear wise without being so, and had said to him: "Truly, it is by this blow that I shall know if you have the patience of a Philosopher." This man—

Of Philosophy. Book II.

### VIII. PROSE.

But so that you do not believe that I am driven by some hatred against Fortune, and that I wage an unjust war against her, I wish to grant her that she sometimes favors men, but it is when she makes them see her inconstancy. Perhaps you do not understand my discourse. It is a strange thing that I cannot express what I desire; nevertheless, here is my opinion. 

Ill Fortune <note>Adversity or "bad luck."</note> is more useful to men than that which seems to be happy; for while the latter always feigns in order to deceive, the former confesses her fickleness through her changes. The one deceives, the other instructs; the former fetters the spirit with appearances of true good, and the latter delivers it through the experience of a false bliss <note>original: "beati-tude"</note>. 

And thus you see that one <note>Good Fortune</note> always full of wind, slippery and blind in the knowledge of what she is; on the contrary, this one <note>Ill Fortune</note> appears somber, composed, and prudent. Finally, good Fortune turns men away from the possession of the true good by her caresses, and ill Fortune pushes them toward it by her adversities. 

Do you count for nothing that this Fortune, which you consider so austere and so troublesome, has revealed the distinction of your true Friends? It is she who has made you discern their faces; in withdrawing, she has taken away her own followers, and has left you those who, without pretense, belong to you. How much would you have paid for this knowledge when you were happy in your own opinion? I pray you, seek your worldly goods no longer; you possess the greatest of them in the knowledge of your true friends.

<vocab>Philosophy, Fortune, inconstancy, ill Fortune, good Fortune, true good, Friends</vocab>

<summary>Lady Philosophy argues that adversity (Ill Fortune) is more beneficial than prosperity because it reveals the truth about the world's fickleness and identifies one's true friends. She concludes that Boethius's loss of wealth is balanced by the discovery of who truly loves him.</summary>
<keywords>Fortune, adversity, friendship, true good, inconstancy, Lady Philosophy, Boethius</keywords>

## VIII. POETRY.

*If the seasons in their return*
*Maintain their rights of precedence:*
*If the Sun presides over the day,*
*If the Moon, his sister, shines in his absence.*

*If the respectful Ocean*
*Does not depart from the bed of its waves,*
*For fear that its prideful surges*
*Might hide from our eyes half of its World.*

*It is love that creates these harmonies,*
*Who places its Empire in the Heavens,*
*Who holds the Sea within its shores,*
*And who creates this sweet air that the earth breathes.*

*Should these movements, so diverse,*
*Slacken within nature;*
*One would see the Universe dissolve,*
*And follow, abandoned, the whims of fate and chance.*

*All these Beings who are united*
*By the knot of a holy alliance,*
*Seeing all these harmonies ended,*
*Would no longer have the sweet fruit of their understanding.* <note>The original "intelligence" here refers to the harmonious communication or "agreement" between different parts of the created world.</note>

*Love unites the Nations,*
*Removing the bitterness <note>original: "fiel" or gall, representing hatred or resentment.</note> from their hearts;*
*Love nourishes the passions*
*With that innocent fire which creates marriages.*

*Love is the law of friends:*
*Oh, how desirable it would be for us*
*To see our hearts also submitted*
*To the law that Heaven always finds lovely.*

## I. PROSE.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring intricate floral and vine patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

SHE had already finished these beautiful verses, 
so much so that their sweetness still ravished me: 
returning then a little from the admiration 
of so many wonders, I cried out: 
Sovereign Consolation of souls 
troubled by anxieties, you have so relieved me, 
both by the weight of your beautiful maxims <note>original: "sentences," referring to authoritative philosophical aphorisms or moral truths.</note>, and by the 
charms of your pleasant melody, that I feel 
strong enough against the attacks of Fortune. And 
therefore I desire at this hour with passion, the 
same remedies that I was avoiding just recently with such diligence.
Your attention, and your silence (replied Philosophy) 
have made it known to me: thus I was expecting 
this disposition, or to speak more truly, I 
was placing it in your mind. Those which remain are a little 
bitter to the mouth, but they are sweet to the stomach: 
they irritate the taste, but they delight the heart. You 
confess that the desire to hear my discourse has moved you; 
by what raptures would you not be surprised, if you 
knew the place where I have begun to lead you? B. 
What then is your design? P. None other than to 
show you the true felicity <term>felicity: the state of supreme happiness and the ultimate "Good" that Philosophy promises to reveal.</term> of which you have had some 
dreams, without your spirit—occupied by sensory 
images—being able to fix its thought on its true nature.

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues Philosophy’s explanation of the "Highest Good." She begins to categorize the various paths humans take in their pursuit of happiness, distinguishing between the true end and the mistaken means used to reach it.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>of Philosophy. Book III.</header>

They are different in their means, but they all converge in the pursuit of one and the same end, which is felicity. Now, properly speaking, felicity is nothing other than that which satisfies the desire of the one who possesses it; it is a good that encompasses all other goods. Nothing can be lacking to it; otherwise, it would not be the sovereign <note>original: "le souuerain," referring to the "summum bonum" or Highest Good.</note>, because it would still leave something outside of itself to be desired. From this I conclude that beatitude <note>original: "beatitude," a state of perfect blessedness or spiritual joy.</note> is a state perfected in every respect, and a gathering of everything that is good. This is what all men strive for, as I have said, even though they walk by diverse paths; for their natural inclination leads them to seek this good, but error turns them away from it.

There are some who, making this felicity consist in having need of nothing, try to amass riches. Others, judging that what is worthy of reverence is the sovereign good, strive through dignities <term>dignities: high offices, ranks, or titles of honor.</term> to earn the respect of their fellow citizens. Some others, unable to imagine this perfect beatitude except in absolute power, employ all their industry to recognize no Master, or if they have one, to approach him as closely as possible. But those who passionately love glory devote themselves to the exercise of arms or the bar <note>original: "le barreau," referring to the legal profession or the practice of law in the courts.</note> in order to acquire it. Those who place happiness in joy and pleasures believe that delight <note>original: "volupté," referring to sensual pleasure or physical gratification.</note> is the only good for men. There are others who mix these goods, like those who desire riches, relating their use to the enjoyment of pleasures or power; or those who make power serve the desire for money or reputation, wishing to be powerful only to become rich or illustrious. It is therefore in these designs that all...

<summary>Philosophy defines true happiness (felicity) as a self-sufficient state that satisfies all desires and then lists the common but misguided paths—wealth, status, power, fame, and pleasure—that men follow to find it.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Philosophy’s analysis of human motivations. Having previously listed the common paths to happiness (riches, honors, power, glory, and pleasure), she now explains how these goals relate to one another and to the concept of the "Sovereign Good."</meta>

# The Consolation

human life revolves: I mean to say that all their care is to possess the goodwill of the people, in order to be glorious, and to have a wife and children, in order to be content. As for friends, it is a good so holy and so august, that it must not be ranked among those of Fortune, but placed with that of virtue. All the rest is done either for power or for pleasure <note>original: "volupté," usually referring to physical or sensual delight.</note>. It is now easy to relate the perfections of the body to what we have said, since strength and stature concern power, while beauty and vigor concern glory and pleasures. It is only by these attractions that felicity makes itself desired, because the sovereign good is nothing other than what everyone seeks. But we have already established that the sovereign good was beatitude. Felicity being, then, only that which excites the greatest desires, you have before your eyes the image of human happiness, having in view riches and honors, power, glory, and pleasure. Epicurus <note>Epicurus (341–270 BC) was a Greek philosopher who taught that the greatest good was to seek tranquility and freedom from bodily pain.</note>, stopping at this last one, established the sovereign good there, because all other goods serve the contentment of the mind. I return to men, who truly pursue beatitude, but like those who search for their home while full of wine <note>A classic metaphor from Boethius: humanity knows where its "home" (true happiness) is by instinct, but is too "intoxicated" by worldly distractions to find the way back.</note>. Does it not seem to you that those are mistaken who strive to have need of nothing? Certainly, only that state can be esteemed happy which has no necessity, and which does not go to borrow the goods of others. Perhaps those who attribute sovereign respect to sovereign felicity do not have true feelings? Far from it: since the designs of men could not be directed toward the acquisition of a good that they <unclear>would judge</unclear> <unclear>unworthy</unclear> of their pursuit. ## of Philosophy. Book III.

should it <note>Philosophy is referring here to "glory" or "reputation."</note> not be ranked among the goods? Why not? Must we believe that what surpasses all things in human esteem is weak and infirm? Can honor be compatible with contempt? I cannot conceive how one can separate what is judged excellent from glory. Who would dare say that happiness was sad, full of anxiety, and subject to the attacks of pain; since in the enjoyment of even the smallest things, one does not want to suffer these inconveniences? If you wish to know what makes men pursue riches, glory, kingdoms, and pleasures with such care, it is because they imagine contentment, reverence, power, and brilliance in their enjoyment. It is therefore the sovereign good <note>The "Sovereign Good" (*Summum Bonum*) refers to the ultimate goal of human existence that provides total satisfaction.</note> that men strive to acquire through so many diverse cares, in which we recognize the force of nature, which animates so many different temperaments with the same inclination.

<meta>This page transitions from the poetic observations of Book III, Meter 2, back into a prose dialogue (Prose 3). Philosophy is now interrogating Boethius to dismantle his lingering attachments to worldly goods, specifically wealth, by demonstrating that they fail to provide the self-sufficiency they promise.</meta>

# of Philosophy. Book III.

If pleasures established a good in which nothing was missing, one would have to admit that their enjoyment would make whoever possessed them happy: but if all this has only the appearance of what it promises, and if it has more true faults than solid perfections, must it not be confessed that it is only a vain image of felicity? <note>original: "felicité" — refers to the state of supreme happiness or blessedness</note> It is from your own mouth that I wish to draw this admission: you have been rich; tell me, while you lived in the abundance of so many comforts, did the displeasure of an insult never trouble your spirit? 

**B.** I cannot remember ever being content to the point of feeling no anxieties. 

**P.** Did that not come from the absence of a good that you would have wished to possess, or from the presence of an evil that you would have desired not to suffer? 

**B.** You say it well. 

**P.** You wished then for the presence of the first, and the absence of the second. 

**B.** I admit it. 

**P.** One suffers the necessity of that which one desires. 

**B.** It is so. 

**P.** He who has need of anything whatever cannot suffice unto himself. <note>This identifies the core philosophical problem: true happiness requires "self-sufficiency," but wealth creates a cycle of dependency and need.</note>

**B.** I could not deny it. 

**P.** Thus, in the possession of so many goods, you had this fault, since you had desires and fears. 

**B.** This truth constrains me. 

**P.** Therefore, riches are not capable of satisfying a heart, which nevertheless they seemed to promise. Here is a consideration that I do not estimate to be of little weight: money has nothing that violence cannot take from him who possesses it. 

**B.** I confess it. 

**P.** You could not have any other sentiment without rejecting the experience that teaches us every day that the weak possess comforts only for the greed <term>avarice: an insatiable desire for wealth, often leading to the exploitation of others</term> of the stronger. From where would so many lawsuits arise if there were no injustices, and if one did not use trickery and force to snatch what belongs to another? 

**B.** Without doubt the world would be without strife

# The Consolation

without dispute. **P.** It must therefore be admitted that he who has money will need external help to preserve it. **B.** That cannot be contradicted. **P.** He would not be obliged to obey this necessity if he possessed nothing of the things that can be lost. **B.** I am still of this opinion. **P.** Here is a strange marvel: riches, which promise to bring man to the point of needing no one, constrain him to make use of everyone. Moreover, let us consider how riches drive away poverty. Perhaps the rich cannot feel hunger; perhaps thirst never affects them; perhaps cold does not dare to freeze the limbs of those who have chests full of crowns. <note>original: "escus" — the écu was a French gold or silver coin, used here as a general term for money.</note> It does not work that way (you will tell me), but they have remedies for all these evils. That is relieving necessity, not curing it. And then, if the desire to have is continual, there is always some lack to repair: I do not say that nature is satisfied with little, and that avarice never has enough; and therefore, if riches cannot drive away indigence, but on the contrary, if they create it, why do you estimate that they can give a good that they do not possess?

## IV. PROSE.

But what then? Do high offices make those who hold them worthy of respect? Do these dignities have the power to plant virtues in the minds of those who are honored by them, and to purge them of their vices? Certainly, it happens all too often that magistracies <note>Public offices or positions of authority in the government.</note> serve more to showcase malice than to correct it. This is why we find cause to condemn the unjust usurpation of these offices by the wicked; it was this same feeling that gave Catullus <note>Gaius Valerius Catullus (c. 84–54 BCE), a Roman poet known for his biting wit.</note> the courage to call Nonius an "abscess," <note>original: "Apostume" — a swelling or tumor; a term of deep contempt for a corrupt official.</note> even though he was seated in the ivory chair <note>original: "chaire d’yuoire" — the *sella curulis*, a traditional seat of honor for high-ranking Roman magistrates.</note> of the Senators. Do you not see what blame honors bring to those who do not know how to use them, since all their brilliance serves only to shine a light on how unworthy they are?

You might doubt this truth, if all the miseries that afflict you could have made you consent to share the honor of the consulship with Decoratus, <note>Decoratus was an official under the Ostrogothic King Theodoric; Boethius viewed him as a corrupt sycophant.</note> whose buffoonish and malicious spirit you knew well? It is impossible to judge that such a man deserves respect by reason of the honor he holds, when we judge him unworthy even of holding that honor. But if you saw a wise man, you could not refuse him the reverence he deserves, nor believe him incapable of a wisdom that you would admire in him—no, you could not. And the reason for this is that virtue has a certain splendor which does not allow those endowed with it to remain hidden. And because the hon-
<meta>catchword: honors</meta>

<summary>Philosophy argues that high political offices do not grant respect or virtue to the unworthy, but instead expose their flaws. She distinguishes between the hollow "dignity" of an office and the inherent, visible splendor of true virtue.</summary>
<keywords>political office, dignity, virtue, Catullus, Nonius, Decoratus, Roman Senate, corruption vs. wisdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Lady Philosophy regarding the vanity of high offices. She has just mentioned Decoratus, a man of low character who held high office, to illustrate that rank does not bestow virtue.</meta>

popular honors do not have this effect, it is easy to conclude that they do not even possess enough beauty or merit for themselves, a point that should be considered attentively. For if a person is all the more contemptible when many people despise them, then the <term>great charges<note>High-ranking public offices or administrative positions.</note></term>—which show the **Wicked** to more people without making them worthy of respect—only expose them to greater contempt. And to tell the truth, this is not without reason, since the wicked return the favor to these <term>dignities<note>The Latin *dignitates* refers to the honors and titles associated with high office.</note></term> by staining them with their own shame and infamy.

But so that you may not be unaware that these apparent greatnesses are not at all capable of giving true merit, weigh this consideration: If someone, after having been <term>Consul<note>The highest elected political office in the Roman Republic and an honorary title of great prestige in Boethius's time.</note></term> several times, found himself among barbarians, would he be honored by them? If these offices had such an effect by their very nature, then no matter how savage a people might be, they would not fail to show him the marks of respect due to his rank—just as fire gives off heat everywhere, because there is no country where it is not hot. 

Since greatness does not have this advantage by its own nature, but only through the opinion of men, those who possess these titles have no sooner arrived among peoples with different views than they lose the esteem of their nation. This is what happens among strangers. Yet it is not even as if their glory is constant in the very place where it is in fashion. Do you believe that this opinion of greatness lasts forever? The <term>Prefecture<note>The office of the Praetorian Prefect, once the most powerful administrative and judicial position in the Empire.</note></term>, which was the great vanity of former times, is now nothing but a name and a burden that is hated today. In the past, it was a distinguished honor to have care of the <term>management of grain<note>original: "mesnage des bleds" — referring to the *Praefectus Annonae*, the official responsible for the food supply of Rome.</note></term>; yet is there anything more despised in the age in which we live? One must recognize the truth of what I <unclear>said</unclear> shortly before: that everything which has no <unclear>proper</unclear>

that it also has some miseries? However great the extent of a **Monarchy** may be, every **King** is respected by only a single People, and remains unknown to many others; and from this side come his sorrows, just as he once drew his joy from his power. From this one can conclude that the share <term>Princes<note>In this context, a general term for any high-ranking sovereign ruler.</note></term> have in misfortunes is greater than the share they take in prosperity. That <term>Tyrant<note>A reference to Dionysius of Syracuse, who illustrated the dangers of power to his courtier Damocles.</note></term> was not without wit who represented the anxieties of a King by the fears that a sword, held by a single thread over his head, would cause him. Tell me, do you believe a man is powerful who desires what he [already] possesses <note>The text suggests a paradox: a powerful man is never truly satisfied because he is always aware of the limitations of his current holdings.</note>? Do you consider him powerful who has a great number of <term>Guards<note>original: "Estaffiers"; specifically henchmen or armed attendants who follow a master.</note></term> at his side, yet who fears the very people he makes tremble, and who can only appear fearsome through the misery of his slaves? What remains for me to say about the favorites of Princes, since they themselves do not receive what one expects from their goodwill, and since their authority has often brought them low through a sudden disgrace, or crushed them by its own ruin? All the favor that **Nero** showed to his Master **Seneca** was to let him choose the manner of his death; **Antonin** exposed **Papinian** <note>Aemilius Papinianus, a famous Roman jurist executed by Emperor Caracalla (referred to here as Antonin).</note> to the swords of his soldiers, even though he had possessed for himself a <unclear>portion</unclear> of the Court's favors. It is true that both men meditated on leaving their high status, and even that **Seneca** tried to surrender his wealth to his Disciple <note>Nero, who was Seneca's student before becoming Emperor.</note> and to seek rest away from the dealings of the great world; but the misfortune that was dragging them toward the precipice did not permit them to complete this plan. ## V. PROSE.

Regarding glory, who can be unaware that it is often empty, and sometimes even shameful? And therefore the Tragedian <note>original: "le Tragique"; referring to the Greek playwright Euripides, specifically a line from his play *Andromache*.</note> has good reason to exclaim: "O glory, o glory, how marvelous is your power to puff up a thousand little men to a greatness beyond measure!" Have not many acquired a fine reputation through the unjust praises of the Common People? Is there anything more deserving of blame? For he who is praised merely to please him <note>original: "par complaisance"; implies flattery or saying what someone wants to hear rather than the truth.</note> ought to blush at such praise.

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 81 regarding the emptiness of worldly glory. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a rhetorical question about whether praise given out of flattery can truly be called praise by reason.</meta>

82 The Consolation

...by reason? If virtues require such recommendation, what advantage does the wise man gain, who measures his merit not by the false opinion of the common people, but by the true testimony of his conscience? If it is a good and laudable action to have extended the glory of one's name, then it is a just reproach not to have done so. But as there are many nations (as I noted just a moment ago) <note>Philosophy refers here to her previous point that the Roman Empire, though vast, is a mere point in the universe, and many distant peoples have never heard of even the most famous Romans.</note> which, due to the distance of places, cannot know a person's fame, it happens that he who appears to you full of glory and splendor, being unseen by half the world, is esteemed as having no luster at all. 

And even if that were not the case, I could not value a reputation that is not supported by the judgment of merit and cannot be preserved by fame alone. Who can be ignorant—unless they are stupid or blind—of how vain nobility is? If one considers its luster, it belongs to others, since nobility, properly speaking, is nothing other than praise that our ancestors <term>Ancestres: The original French term "ancestres" refers to one's lineage and the hereditary status passed down through generations.</term> acquired by their valor. If nobility consists only in the recommendation of others, then undoubtedly even the wicked will appear noble if flattery bestows praises upon them. Therefore, if you are not commendable in yourself, I do not believe that the splendor of others can make you illustrious. If the nobility of a good lineage is useful in any way, I believe it is only through the strict obligation it imposes: the duty not to degenerate from the virtue of one's ancestors.

## VII. PROSE.

What shall I say of sensual pleasures? The desire for them is full of anxiety, and the enjoyment of them is full of regret. Who can conceive of how many pains and diseases they inflict upon the body, which they seem to flatter <note>In this context, "flatter" means to stroke or pamper, suggesting that pleasure deceives the body with a false sense of well-being.</note>? This is the only fruit one gathers from the use of pleasures. Whoever wishes only to remember the end of his debaucheries will know with how much bitterness pleasure is mixed. And then, if pleasures could truly make one happy, I do not see why beasts would not be happy as well as man, since all their inclinations and their...

# The Consolation

minds unite in the enjoyment of pleasures; whereas those of a rational creature would never be able to fully gather themselves into such a brutal <term>Brutal: In this context, it refers to the nature of a "brute" or animal, lacking the higher faculty of reason.</term> trade. The contentment one receives from a wife and a family is rational, but it is only too true (in the opinion of someone) that nature gives executioners to fathers by giving them children. It would be a superfluous task to try to communicate other knowledge to you than that which you hold from your own experience. I cannot reject the beautiful words of Euripides on this subject, when he said that he who had no children was happy through his own misfortune <note>original: "estoit heureux par son propre malheur." This refers to a passage in Euripides' play *Andromache*, suggesting that while childlessness is a misfortune, it spares one the greater agony of seeing children suffer or die.</note>.

### Of Philosophy. Book III.

Do you wish to have dignities <note>In this context, "dignities" refers to high-ranking social offices or titles of honor.</note>? You must act the slave before the one who bestows them; and to surpass others in honor, one must stoop to an infinity of shameful humiliations. Does power stir your desire? You will be exposed to the perfidies and betrayals of your subjects. Do you seek glory? You lose your peace of mind. Does your inclination lead you to the enjoyment of filthy pleasures? Who would be so base as not to despise rendering such shameful service to their own flesh?

Regarding those who prize the goods of the body, it is evident that their support is weak. Can you ever surpass elephants in size, or bulls in strength? Perhaps your swiftness will outrun that of tigers. Look upon the expanse, the stability, and the speed of the Heavens <note>Philosophy refers to the celestial spheres which, in the medieval and early modern worldview, were considered the most perfect and enduring parts of creation.</note>, and cease to admire lowly things. If the beauties that touch your eyes seem more agreeable than those of this great work <note>The "great work" refers to the Universe or the Divine order.</note>, you are at least obliged to admire its governance.

Leaving this providence to your consideration, I must pause a moment on the charms of that beauty which you love so strongly: Ah! how inconstant it is, and how the flowers of spring are like it, in that they appear beautiful and fade almost in the same moment. If we had the eyes of a lynx (as Aristotle said) to look through objects, would not this body of Alcibiades <note>Alcibiades was a famous Athenian statesman and general, renowned in the ancient world for his extraordinary physical beauty.</note>, whose exterior is so ravishing, appear hideous? It is not, therefore, your perfection that makes you beautiful, but the weakness of the eyes that look upon you. Nevertheless, it matters not; I allow

On the contrary, he who directs all his desires toward power dissipates his wealth, despises pleasures, and cares very little for an honor that is separated from it. You see clearly enough how many things a person of this inclination lacks, since very often they lack the necessities, and an infinity of cares tear them apart; from which it happens that, being unable to rid themselves of these disturbances, they cease to be powerful—the very thing they primarily sought. One can easily discourse upon honors, glory, and pleasures; for as all these things have a single nature, whoever wishes to divide them will not even attain the one they pursue. If someone desires them all at once, do they desire true happiness? And if they can acquire them separately, will they find in them a good that these things cannot guarantee? **B.** Not at all. **P.** It is therefore not in their enjoyment that one must seek happiness. **B.** Truth itself could not speak better. **P.** You now know the concept and the causes of false beatitude <note>original: "beatitude"; here meaning a state of supreme, yet in this case illusory, blessedness</note>; only cast your eyes in the other direction, and it will be easy for you to perceive that which is its opposite. **B.** I believe one would have to be blind not to see it, and you have declared it clearly enough by the opposition you have made. # The Consolation

...is capable of making a man content, so that he will possess happiness in its entirety.

**P.** You are wise (my dear pupil <note>original: "nourrifson"; literally "nursling," a term of endearment for a student</note>) to have added this limitation.

**B.** And what limitation?

**P.** Do you believe there is anything among corruptible things <note>Meaning transitory or perishable earthly goods as opposed to eternal ones.</note> capable of making a person happy?

**B.** You have instructed me so well that it is impossible for me to hold such false opinions.

**P.** It is therefore certain that created things possess nothing but a vain image of blessedness <note>original: "beatitude"</note>, and goods that are only so in appearance <note>original: "en monstre"; meaning a hollow display or a mere show lacking substance.</note>.

**B.** I share your sentiments.

**P.** Since you recognize perfect happiness, and outward appearance can no longer deceive you, it is now appropriate to show you how you may become truly happy.

**B.** That is what I have long desired.

**P.** But if you are not unaware of what our Plato says in his *Timaeus* <note>A dialogue by Plato that explores the creation of the universe; Boethius heavily incorporates these Platonic ideas here.</note>—that in even the smallest undertakings, one must implore the help of the Gods—what do you judge we ought to do to find the dwelling-place of this sovereign good?

**B.** Without doubt, we must have recourse to the Father of all things, without whom no beginning is properly made.

**P.** That is well said; let us offer Him our prayers.

You who, to create all things, use only yourself,
And who have no motive but your extreme love,
Without anything from the outside forcing your will
To communicate to us the effect of its goodness.
It is you who, as a pattern <note>original: "patron"; in this context, it refers to the Platonic "Ideas" or the divine blueprint used by the Creator to shape the world.</note> for so many beautiful things,

All our charms are taken from your ravishing features,

Perfectly whole yourself, you perfect these diverse members,

You make the friendships of the Elements <note>According to ancient science, the four elements (fire, air, water, earth) were in a state of "discordant harmony," kept in balance by divine power.</note> easy

In order to temper the cold with the heat,

The purest half of the fire that matter

Equally animates so many diverse members.
Spirit which, divided into two spherical globes <note>This refers to the Platonic idea that the World Soul is distributed in two circles—the circle of the "Same" (the celestial equator) and the circle of the "Other" (the ecliptic or path of the planets).</note>
Whose movement makes its oblique returns,
Joining the same place from where it had departed,
And returning into itself without having left it,
Meditates on all the cares of this profound soul,
Which rises from the hollow of the mass of the world,
Imitating within itself the same movement
That the starry fires have in the Firmament.
The most noble Spirits and common souls
Receive from your hand their diverse fortunes.

## X. PROSE.

Having recognized the essence of the true and the apparent good, it is appropriate to declare in what perfection consists. To create a discourse whose foundations are solid, one must first know if there is any good of this quality in nature, for fear that our imagination might lose itself in its own fictions. For my part, I am of the opinion of those who assert it; and I believe, unless one is stupid, that one cannot deny this source of all other goods, since a good is only imperfect because of the deficiency it has compared to that which is complete <note>original: "accomply"; meaning a state of being fully realized or perfected.</note>. From this, one must gather that if there is some goodness in a certain category <note>original: "ordre"; referring to a class or rank of being.</note>, one must recognize one in that same category that has no defect: otherwise, it is impossible (without presupposing perfection) to conceive how a good could be imperfect. Reason

### On Philosophy. Book. III. ###

...of this is that nature did not begin with its smallest works; rather, conducting its designs from a beautiful beginning toward an entirely opposite end, it <margin>*</margin> has, so to speak, allowed its productions to conclude in the smallest effects of its power <note>Philosophy is arguing for a "top-down" creation, where perfection exists first and the lesser, "perishable" things of our world are the final, diminished results of that divine power.</note>. And therefore, if perishable goods provide some sort of budding and imperfect happiness, one is forced to recognize a happiness that lacks nothing.

**B.** This sequence of reasoning is very judicious.

**P.** Look now where this happiness is found. The belief of rational spirits is that God alone is the principle of all good. For if one can conceive of nothing better than God, and since God possesses all the good that one can receive, the reason is as evident as it is necessary that He has within Himself the true good; and if it were otherwise, He would not be the sovereign Monarch <note>original: "Monarque"; a common early modern term used here to emphasize God's absolute authority over the laws of the universe.</note> of the world, since something would precede Him, both in perfection of goodness and in order of time, because the perfect always precedes that which is not. And thus, to relieve our minds of the burden of an infinite argument, we must admit that God is full of goods and perfections, and consequently that He possesses sovereign happiness <note>original: "felicité"; often used interchangeably with "beatitude" to describe the highest state of well-being.</note>.

**B.** This discourse cannot suffer any opposition.

**P.** But so that your consent is not subject to any suspicion of lightheartedness, I pray you to consider in what sense I have said that He possessed all goods. Beware of thinking that God receives this good from the outside, as if the nature of the happiness possessed were different from the one who possesses it. Because if God borrowed His goods from someone, the giver would have some advantage over the receiver, and thus our discourse would contradict itself, since we recognize nothing <unclear>more</unclear> excellent than God. And if God and happiness <unclear>do not have</unclear> any other distinction...

<meta>This page continues the logical argument from Book III, Prose X of Boethius’s "The Consolation of Philosophy." Having established that God is the source of all perfection, Philosophy now proceeds to prove that God and perfect happiness are not merely related, but are in fact the same essence.</meta>

### The Consolation ###

...than that which our mind places there, I leave it to anyone who wishes to guess who joined them. Furthermore, that which is different from another is not the same thing from which it differs; and therefore, that which differs from the true good is not the true good, which one cannot think of God without blasphemy. The reason for this is that nothing can be more perfect than its principle <note>In this philosophical context, a "principle" is the source or origin. Logic dictates that a cause must be equal to or greater than its effect.</note>, and thus if I confess that one thing is the cause of all others, it must likewise be admitted that it will be sovereign happiness.

**B.** It is certain.

**P.** We have likewise shown that God was the sovereign good; and therefore He is beatitude <note>original: "beatitude"; a state of supreme, divine happiness.</note>. Let us see now if the impossibility of two sovereign goods, which are in opposition, will not confirm this truth. One cannot doubt that goods which are contrary are not the same; therefore, if there is any of this nature, it will not be perfect, since the perfection that makes the other dissimilar to it is lacking. If it is not perfect, it is not the sovereign; if it is the sovereign good, nothing will be contrary to it by the difference of a merit that it does not have. We have shown that God and happiness <note>original: "felicité"</note> were the sovereign good: therefore sovereign beatitude is nothing other than the sovereign Divinity.

**B.** One could not better clarify the truth nor treat God with more respect than by conferring this advantage upon His greatness.

**P.** I wish to behave toward you like the Geometricians <note>Philosophy refers to the "corollaries" or additional deductions that mathematicians add to a proven theorem.</note>, who always add some things beyond their demonstrations. Men are happy by the acquisition of beatitude; beatitude is nothing other than Divinity; therefore men are happy by the acquisition of Divinity. But just as Wisdom makes the wise, and Justice the just, for the same reason Divinity makes—

<meta>This page continues Philosophy’s argument that God and happiness are identical. The previous page ended with Philosophy explaining that sovereign beatitude is the same as the sovereign Divinity. This page begins by concluding that humans can become "gods" through their participation in this divine happiness, then moves to examine whether the components of happiness (power, honor, etc.) are distinct parts or a single unified essence.</meta>

# of Philosophy. Book III. #

...makes Gods; Therefore, he who is happy is God, for although there is only one by essence, nothing prevents there being many by participation <note>Philosophy is using a Platonic concept here: while there is only one absolute God, humans can "participate" in divinity by achieving true happiness, effectively becoming god-like.</note>. Here is that excellent point which had to be added; I believe there is nothing more ravishing than that which can still be reasonably joined to what we have said. Since there are many parts which compose the perfection of this happiness, this doubt might be formed: whether there is one among them which particularly is its essence, and to which all others relate as properties. We have shown that beatitude <note>original: "beatitude"; the state of supreme blessedness.</note> was the sovereign good; tell me now: a sufficiency without defect, a power without weakness, a respect without contempt, a glory without dishonor, a contentment without displeasure—is this not beatitude? Does it not seem to you that these are rather its parts than the whole? I wish to make myself understood: if all these things were portions of happiness, they would undoubtedly have some distinction between them, since the nature of a body is to receive its completion from several pieces differing from one another. Abundance, power, reverence, glory, and pleasure, since they have its entire nature.

**B.** Your discourse gives me satisfaction, but its sequel does not leave me without desire.

**P.** Unless you reject the truth, you cannot deny that power, honor, and all other things are only desired in consideration of the good that we believe to be in them. The good is therefore the source of all desires: and certainly it is not possible to wish for that which does not possess its merit; on the contrary, even that which has only the appearance of it makes itself loved, from which it must be concluded that goodness <note>original: "bonté"</note> is the root of everything that is sought; and as

This page concludes that logical proof and transitions into a poem that invites those burdened by worldly desires to find their rest in this divine truth.</meta>

...things that make others desirable through the goodness they impart to them, one must grant that they themselves have the power to be primarily desired. Just as if someone wants to go on horseback for their health, they do not seek the motion of the horse so much as the effect of its movement <note>Philosophy uses this analogy to show that we often desire a means (the horse's movement) only for the sake of the end goal (health).</note>. Therefore, all things being desirable for the good that is, or is imagined to be, within them, that which provides the motive for the desire must itself be desirable. There is no reason to contradict this truth, and I believe that everyone willingly agrees that goodness and beatitude <note>original: "beatitude"; supreme blessedness or happiness</note> share the same essence; and therefore, since God and beatitude are but one thing, one must believe that the nature of God resides in the true good.

---

## XI. PROSE

**E**verything you have said is so solidly supported that it is impossible for me not to agree with you. **P.** <note>Philosophy</note> What opinion will you have of my courtesy if I reveal to you the nature of this true good? **B.** <note>Boethius</note> To tell the truth, my gratitude <note>original: "ressentiment"; in 17th-century French, this often meant a deep feeling of appreciation or a "realization" rather than the modern "resentment."</note> will be infinite, if it is reasonable, since I cannot know it without knowing God. **P.** Always presupposing what we have already said, it will be easy for me to show it to you. Have I not clearly enough demonstrated that these things for which men have such great desires are not true goods, since they are at odds with one another? Because the goodness of one is not the goodness of the other, their perfection is not complete—

<summary>Philosophy concludes a poem about the blinding nature of earthly jewels and begins a logical proof with Boethius, arguing that since worldly desires are fragmented and contradictory, they cannot constitute the "True Good."</summary>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues Philosophy’s argument from the end of the previous page, where she began to demonstrate that the "True Good" is synonymous with unity. The word "plie" at the top is the conclusion of the word "accomplie" (accomplished) from the preceding page.</meta>

->98<-
->**The Consolation**<-

[accom]plished. From this discourse it was easy enough to conclude that the solid good <note>The "true good" or the ultimate happiness Philosophy is defining.</note> is only achieved through the concurrence and union of all their benefits, and that if all these things are not gathered into one, they do not have enough merit to be desired. From this knowledge, we draw a truth: that there is no good in division, and that everything, by becoming one through union, becomes good through unity. And as nothing is preserved except through union, so too nothing is ruined except through division. 

We see an image of this in the nature of an animal, which only subsists through the alliance of form and matter, which are no sooner separated than this nature is corrupted <note>Refers to the moment of death, when the soul (form) leaves the body (matter).</note>. The same can still be observed in the figure of the human body, which possesses beauty only in the assembly of its various parts, which are no sooner divided than they are no longer what they were. Whoever takes the trouble to reason thus about other things will find that they maintain themselves in unity and lose themselves through multiplicity. 

This being so, do you believe that the desire to no longer exist is natural? Certainly, if we look at the inclination of those animals that have, in some sense, a will, we must admit that there is none among them that does not desire its preservation, since each one flees and moves away with all its power from its ruin. As for herbs and plants, there is no reason to doubt it, seeing each of them born in places where their nourishment is easiest and where withering is least to be feared. Some germinate in the middle of fields, others can only live on mountains. These grow in lakes, those draw their life from rocks. Some even produce a fertile crop from sterile sands. But if someone attempts to transplant them, it is only to

### Of Philosophy. Book III.
->99<-
<meta>Digitized by Google</meta>

...to be seen soon withering. It is thus that nature imprints its desires for self-preservation upon all beings. Shall I say that the roots draw—like mouths hidden in the earth—vigor into the branches and the bark? Shall I speak of the artifice of nature which encloses the marrow <note>In this context, marrow refers to the pith or internal heartwood of a tree.</note> in the middle of the trunk, as it is more delicate, and spreads the bark on the outside as it is more capable of suffering the injuries of the air? 

Add to this the care nature takes to multiply plants through the abundance of their seeds, from which it is easy to understand that her design is not only to make them live for a time, but indeed to make them immortal through an ever-continued succession. 

Pass now to things that have no life, and you will see that they follow the same desire. Why does the flame always tend upward by its lightness? Why does the weight of the earth precipitate it downward, if it is not because these locations and movements are like their natural desires? No one can deny that everything draws its preservation from what is harmonious with it, just as their ruin comes only from what is contrary to it. Even stones are not insensitive to the care of preserving themselves, since all their parts cling to one another; air and water allow themselves to be divided without resistance, but they reunite without difficulty. As for fire, the person has yet to be born who could cut it <note>This reflects the classical belief that fire, as a pure element, was indivisible, unlike solids or fluids.</note>. 

I am not speaking here of the movements of man, which are regulated by reasoned discourse <note>Meaning rational thought or deliberation.</note>, but only of those necessary movements which have no other guidance than the powerful impression of nature. Such as nourishment, which is processed within us without our reason, and sleep, which does not require our conscious knowledge. Inasmuch as the desire to exist forever does not come

<header>The Consolation</header>

...to animals of a will they do not possess, but only of the principles of their being: since we see commonly enough that reasoned discourse makes us accept death, which our natural inclination flees, and on the contrary, the will often moderates those pleasures that nature always seeks as the only means of making itself immortal. After so many truths have been clarified, it seems to me that one can no longer doubt that the providence of God has given all these instincts to His creatures. Now, in this very fact that all things desire to perpetuate themselves, they desire **unity**, since nothing can subsist by division. Do you remember that I showed that what is **one** is that very thing which is **good**? Therefore, each thing seeking unity seeks the good: from which one can draw this definition of the good, if one says that it is what all creatures seek.

**B.** One could not speak with more judgment; for without this bond that they find in unity, they would return to nothingness <note>original: "neant"; referring to the state of non-existence from which things were created.</note>, from which they emerged. If they tend toward some end, it is toward this **sovereign good** <term>sovereign good: the "summum bonum," or the highest, most complete form of happiness and virtue</term> of which you have spoken to me.

**P.** I rejoice, my dear Disciple, that you are beginning to know the truth; in acknowledging your instruction, you confess the ignorance you previously had regarding our final end. Stand firm in this belief: that what the whole world desires is their final end, and because there is no other end than blessedness <note>original: "beatitude"</note>, it must be held for certain that this final end is the sovereign good.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book III, Prose 12. Philosophy is helping Boethius recover his "forgotten" knowledge. Having established that happiness and the Supreme Good are one and the same (and are found in God), she now asks Boethius how he believes the universe is governed.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>
<header>The Consolation</header>
<meta>Catchword: I had</meta>

...will not be very far from what you confessed you did not know. Tell me, I pray you, who governs this great world? B. That is what I have wished to learn from you. P. Have you not confessed to me that it is guided by the providence <term>providence: the protective care of God as a spiritual power</term> of God alone? B. I have never doubted it; and if you please, I will briefly produce the reasons that have given me this belief. Without doubt, the great world could not have assembled so many different parts into a single body if some sovereign power had not united so many contraries: and the diversity of so many creatures would dissolve this union if He who allied them did not maintain them in the harmony <note>original: "accord"</note> He gave them. Truly, the order of nature would not be so precise, its movements so composed, and the sequence of reasons so constant, if there were not someone who disposed these vicissitudes, and who regulated these changes without being changed Himself. This power which has such beautiful effects is called God among all nations, although perhaps they do not all agree in the knowledge of His nature. P. Since you have such healthy opinions, there remains very little for me to do to put you in the enjoyment of happiness <note>original: "felicité"</note>, and to set you on the path toward your true Homeland <note>In Boethian philosophy, the "Patrie" or Homeland is the spiritual realm or the mind of God from which the soul originated.</note>. But let us keep our thoughts on the subject we are treating. You know well that we have included sufficiency <term>sufficiency: the quality of being self-adequate or needing nothing else</term> within bliss <term>bliss: "beatitude," or the highest state of well-being</term>. It is therefore evident that He has no need of external help to govern the world; otherwise, He would not have this sufficiency that we have attributed to Him. Thus God guides the world by Himself, and as God is the true good, it is goodness that governs all things. Here is the knot that binds all Beings. Here is the rudder <note>original: "gouuernail"</note> that handles them. B. I was waiting for you to make this speech to me, and as ignorant as I am,

exit. <note>original: "sortie." This word concludes the sentence from the previous page regarding the powerlessness of evil.</note> Is it thus permitted to move within the circle of divine perfections, and to multiply in so many ways that which is simple <note>In medieval theology, "divine simplicity" is the idea that God is not made of parts; His goodness, His power, and His existence are all the same thing.</note> in every respect? Sometimes, beginning with beatitude <note>A state of supreme blessedness or perfect happiness.</note>, you said it was the sovereign good, the perfection of which you placed in God; and then, as if you had retraced your steps, you asserted that God was the sovereign good and perfect happiness, from which you drew the consequence that no one could be happy unless they were God. You added to this that the nature of God was the essence of beatitude, and that the Good was not different from this unity to which all the desires of creatures are directed. Furthermore, you stated that God used His goodness like reins <note>Continuing the metaphor of God as the driver or pilot of the universe.</note> to guide the world, that all things have an obedience that is not forced, and that evil is nothing. To show that the proofs of these truths were easy for you, without spreading yourself thin toward external matters, you took them from their own nature, establishing one upon the firmness of the other.

**P.** My desire has not been to deceive you, but to instruct you. Here we are, by the grace of God, at the end of a design that had made us implore the help of His goodness. It is the nature of the divine essence not to go out into external things, and to receive nothing foreign, but to turn within itself like a circle, according to the thought of Parmenides <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher who described reality as a perfect, unchanging sphere.</note>. If I have used reasons that are natural to the subject I am treating, and if I have not borrowed them from the outside, you must not be astonished, since you have been able to learn from Plato <note>Philosophy refers to the *Timaeus*, where Plato suggests that our language and arguments should be "akin" to the truths they describe.</note> that the discourses which declare to us the nature of things must be their relatives and have a kinship with them.

Already grieved that he had come there as a savior. <note>This refers to the spirits of the dead being moved by the strange sight of a living man entering their realm to rescue another.</note>

Which Heaven hides from our sight,

Returns it toward the shadows below, <note>Literally "beneath the clouds," representing a return to worldly desires and material distractions.</note>
What he had acquired faces the same ruin.

# BOOK IV

## PROSE I

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring a feline animal, possibly a leopard, standing amidst floral scrolls and foliage.</image-desc>
When Wisdom <note>The personification of Lady Philosophy, the narrator's guide.</note> had finished this agreeable song with a majesty that in no way diminished the sweetness of her expression, and while my sadness was not yet entirely dispelled, I anticipated the intention she had to continue her discourse with these words. "To tell the truth, my good Mistress, everything you have put forward shines with its own light, and is so solidly supported by your powerful reasons, that it is no less difficult to be ignorant of it than to contradict it. I cannot hide the fact that the resentment of a very recent injury <note>The author, Boethius, was a high-ranking Roman official who had been unjustly imprisoned and stripped of his titles.</note> had stripped me of the memory of these beautiful maxims, although it had not erased all knowledge of them from my mind. In order to hide nothing from you of what affects me, here is the principal cause of my distress: Since the Governor of the world <note>Referring to God as the providential ruler.</note> is equitable, why is it that we see evils within it? Or, if His extreme goodness chooses to allow them, why does His Justice leave them unpunished? Judge for yourself what wonder such conduct deserves; but here is a far more reasonable subject for astonishment: when malice triumphs, poor virtue is not only deprived of the rewards of her merit, but the wicked <term>scelerats</term> <note>From the Latin 'sceleratus,' meaning those who are wicked, criminal, or villainous.</note> trample her underfoot, and as if their

<summary>In this opening to Book IV, Boethius acknowledges the truth of Philosophy’s previous arguments but raises the "problem of evil": if a good God rules the world, why does evil exist and why are the virtuous punished while the wicked prosper?</summary>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Boethius and Lady Philosophy from the beginning of Book IV. The narrator has just expressed his deepest grievance: that in a world ruled by a good God, evil exists and often prospers while virtue suffers.</meta>

header: On Philosophy. Book IV.

...crimes were unpayable, virtue is made to stand as security for the punishments that they should suffer. To see such disorders in the state of Him who sees all, of Him whose power is infinite, and of Him who can only will the good, is a misfortune that no one could ever sufficiently lament or wonder at.

**Philosophy.** Truly, if it were as you say, men would never have seen a similar prodigy: would it be possible that in the house of such a wise Master of the house <note>Refers to the Latin *paterfamilias*; a common philosophical metaphor for God's providential care over the universe.</note>, the most worthless vessels would hold the rank of the most precious furniture? It is not so; for if the truths we have established remain firm in your mind, you shall know, by the favor of this great King of whom we speak, that the Good are always powerful and the wicked weak; that Virtues are never without reward, nor crimes without punishment; and that happiness always caresses the people of merit, whereas the wicked receive nothing but the disgraces of Fortune. You shall know many other things which will demonstrate to you the injustice of your complaints and soften their bitterness. And because my instructions have shown you true blessedness and the place of its dwelling, cutting away all that is not necessary, I wish to mark for you the path which will lead you without detours to your home; and to make the journey easier for you, I wish to give wings to your spirit <note>A reference to the Platonic idea of the soul regaining its "wings" to ascend back to the divine realm.</note>, so that you may grant me all the glory of having brought you back to your sweet Homeland <note>original: "Patrie"; in this context, it refers to the celestial origin of the soul rather than a physical country.</note>.

# La Consolation

To behold eternal beauties,
I am sometimes transported to the Heavens;
Then my glorious soul
Braves that last element, <note>In the ancient and medieval world-view, the "last element" refers to the sphere of fire, which was believed to exist just below the moon.</note>
And with a disdainful glance,
Sees the air sink down humbly beneath my feet.

And then passing over that flame,
Whose innocent heat,
Does not even suffer the reproach,
Of changing its color in Heaven.
She walks within the Ecliptic, <note>The circular path of the Sun across the sky.</note>
And following the steps of the Sun,
She shows that this unique one,
Though father of the day, is no longer peerless.

Or indeed joining with that star, <note>A reference to Saturn, which in traditional astrology was considered "pensive" or "cold" because of its slow orbit and distance from the Sun.</note>
Which seems to dream all pensively,
Seeking some disaster for us,
At the first point of its rising,
She follows those lovely dark stars <note>original: "belles brunettes"; a poetic reference to the constellations or the lesser stars that accompany the planets in the night sky.</note>
Which, without causing any noise,
Strive along with the planets
To dissipate the horror of the darkest night.

After this innocent exchange,
She returns like a flash of lightning
To the final Heaven which she crosses
To come to rest at the height of the air:
There, contemplating that powerful Master
Whose slaves are Kings,
She begins to recognize,
That it is His will which gives us our laws.

# Of Philosophy. Book IV.

If your spirit can find its place
Atop this highest element, <note>Referring to the sphere of fire, the highest of the four earthly elements in the medieval cosmos.</note>
By turning your face toward Heaven,
You will reach this wise conclusion:
"It is a heavy and profound ignorance
To desire any other dwelling;
I must always remember,
That I have no country, but the country of God."

But if you wish to see the Earth again,
That somber dwelling-place of night,
Where only the flash of lightning
Makes a brief moment of day and noise,
You will see those arrogant Princes,
Who are served with such endless care,
In the middle of their own Provinces,
To be—though they are Kings—but Slaves and Exiles. <note>Philosophy suggests that rulers who lack virtue are actually prisoners of their own passions and circumstances, far more "exiled" than the virtuous Boethius.</note>

### II. PROSE.

O God, how magnificent Your promises are; I have no doubt, however, that You can fulfill them; I pray You not to let this desire You have awakened in me wither away. 

**P.** <note>Philosophy</note> You must first recognize that the Good are never weak, nor the Wicked powerful, for the one follows from the other. The reason for this is taken from the opposition of good and evil; if one can show the power of the good, the weakness of evil is recognized at once; if the helplessness of evil is evident, the stability of the good appears immediately. But so that this truth is not suspected of being a paradox, <note>In this context, a "paradox" is an idea that contradicts common worldly perception—specifically the idea that powerful-looking villains are actually weak.</note> I wish to establish my proposition. Two things usually concur—

On the previous page, Lady Philosophy began to argue that the Good are inherently powerful while the Wicked are inherently weak. This section explores the relationship between "will" and "power" as the two necessary components for any action.</meta>

<header>The Consolation</header>

—ment <note>original: "ment", likely the suffix of a word such as "uniquement" (only) or "également" (equally) from the previous page.</note> to actions: the will and the power. But they are so closely joined that no effect is ever produced by the first without the help of the second. One never undertakes what one does not desire, and if power is lacking, the will is useless. From this, you may learn of a lack of power in him who does not obtain what he desires; and if you see that another has accomplished something, do not doubt that he willed to do it. From which it is evident that one is powerful in that which one can do, and weak when one cannot. 

Do you remember that I have shown that the inclinations of human wills, however different they may be, all tend toward <term>beatitude: the state of supreme blessedness or perfect happiness which Philosophy argues is the ultimate goal of all human action</term>? Do you remember that happiness can consist only in the Good, and therefore it is impossible to wish for one without desiring the other? You cannot likewise be ignorant that the Good and the Wicked are not contrary in their aim to be happy, even though they are contrary in their way of life. 

But here is a difference between them: it is that the Good are made better by the enjoyment of the Good they seek, while the Wicked, not being good, cannot possess the true Good. One might find it strange that since both have the same pretensions <note>original: "pretentions", here meaning their claims or intentions toward reaching a goal.</note>, they do not have the same success. Why is that? Because some are weak and others are powerful. 

**B.** <note>Boethius</note> Whoever does not grasp these <unclear>truths</unclear> ignores the nature of Beings and does not know the value of reason. 

**P.** <note>Philosophy</note> If two men had the same goal, and one of them came to obtain it through a natural action, and the other only through imitation, which would you judge to be the more powerful?

<summary>Lady Philosophy argues that any action requires both the will to do it and the power to achieve it. She asserts that while both good and evil people seek happiness, only the good have the power to actually achieve it, making the wicked fundamentally weak.</summary>

# On Philosophy. Book IV.

->Digitized by Google<-

...if one person uses their feet, and another, not having the free use of them, employs their hands to run, which of the two will be the more robust? I am inclined to believe that you have enough sense to prefer nature over artifice. There is no man so heavy or so dull that he does not feel the desire for the Good; some seek it through the practice of virtue, others through the excesses of their greed <term>conuoitise: disordered or excessive worldly desire, often translated as "cupidity" or "lust," which Philosophy argues is a misguided attempt to find happiness</term>, which are not the proper means to reach it.

**B.** <note>B. refers to Boethius, the narrator and student</note> I understand your discourse well, and certainly it follows from the propositions I have received that the Good are powerful and the wicked are weak.

**Philosophy:** When the physician begins to hope, it is a sign that nature is helping itself. Since your mind is growing stronger and the difficulties of a tangled truth do not stop you, I wish to show you a great many reasons. Do you not recognize the impotence of wicked men in that they cannot reach the goal where their natural inclination drives them? What would it be if they were deprived of that natural help which almost constrains them to seek happiness? 

Consider now how extreme the weakness of the wicked is! It is not in small things that their powerlessness becomes remarkable, but in the acquisition of true goods. In this, the power of the Good shines with brilliance. For if someone had gone so far that there was no more land left for taking new steps, would you not believe that he was a good walker? Make the same judgment of him who has nothing left to desire, since he possesses everything. This consideration clearly reveals that those who have vices have no power. For I ask you, why do the wicked leave virtue for vice? Does this not come from an ignorance of the Good? Is anything weaker than ignorance? Perhaps they know what deserves their...

# The Consolation

page 114

search, but that their own inclination turns them away from it. Does not a free choice lead them to debauchery? Certainly, in that case, vice does not only make them powerless, but causes them not to be at all; for it is true that those who turn away from the <term>end: the final cause or ultimate purpose (Greek: *telos*); in this context, the "Sovereign Good" or God</term> of all creatures cease to be of their number. This will perhaps seem extravagant <note>In the 17th-century sense of "outrageous" or "absurd."</note> to someone—to say that the wicked, who far outnumber the good, do not exist at all; and nonetheless, it is true. I do not dispute that the wicked exist in some fashion, but that they "exist" in the proper sense of the term is what I cannot grant. 

Just as one calls a corpse a "dead man," and not simply a "man"; likewise I will readily allow that the wicked *are*, but not that they exist absolutely and without limitation. That which does not depart from its nature, precisely speaking, *is*; and that which turns away from its nature, *is not*. One will tell me that villains have power; I admit it, but it comes from weakness, since all their power extends only to evil, which their baseness <note>original: *lascheté*, which can mean cowardice, but here refers to a lack of moral strength or vileness.</note> cannot avoid. 

If evil is nothing <note>Philosophy refers back to an earlier argument (Book III) where she establishes that because God—who can do all things—cannot do evil, then evil must be "nothing" (a lack or privation rather than a positive force).</note>, as we have proved, and the wicked are capable only of evil, they are capable of nothing; and therefore their "virtue" <note>Used here in the sense of "potency" or "power," rather than moral excellence.</note> only serves to reveal their defect. To give you a clearer expression of this truth, do you not remember that I showed there is nothing so strong as the Sovereign Good? Yet it cannot do the evil that the wicked do with too much ease. Therefore, since only the good can do everything, it is evident that those who can only do evil can do nothing. From this it follows that I have shown power was among the desirable things, and that all that merits desire relates to

relates to the true good. There is no mind so brutish as to believe that the power to commit a crime is a good thing; it is therefore not the object of a reasonable desire. Re-examine this discourse now: All power is to be desired; that of vice is not; therefore, vice is not a power. Here is a beautiful saying from Plato: <note>Philosophy refers to a concept from Plato's *Gorgias*, arguing that while the wicked do what pleases them, they do not do what they truly want, which is the Good.</note> only wisdom can do what it desires; malice practices what is easy for it, but it does not achieve everything it undertakes. The wicked truly do what flatters them with the intent of procuring some good for themselves; nevertheless, they will never obtain it, since crimes can never be happy.

### III. P R O S E.

DO you not see now the shame of vice and the glory of virtue? Do you not learn from this that merit is never without reward, nor crimes without punishment? In all things that are undertaken, the end is always the prize; thus the crown <note>original: *la couronne*. Referring to the laurel wreath or crown awarded to winners in Ancient Greek and Roman athletic games, a common metaphor for the goal of life's struggle.</note> is the motive and the reward of the race. We have shown that happiness is the only good for which all human actions are performed; therefore, this same good is the prize of these actions. It is certain that the good cannot be separated from the Good <note>Philosophy is distinguishing here between "the good" (virtuous people) and "the Good" (the ultimate source of happiness/God).</note>, since their goodness is derived only from the union they have with it: therefore, good character never lacks the recognition that justice owes it. Let the storms and tempests beat as much as they wish upon the head of the Wise man, it will always be impossible for them to strike down or wither his crown, since it is certain that the malice of others cannot harm his virtue. For if he took his contentment from an external good <note>original: *bien estranger*. This refers to "Fortune"—things like wealth or status—which are outside of one's own control.</note>, then undoubtedly the very one who had given him the enjoyment of it could also cause its loss. But since a man of virtue is happy only through his own virtues, he only ceases to be so [when he ceases to be virtuous]...

<header>of Philosophy. Book IV.</header>

...more when he begins to be vicious. Furthermore, if a reward is only desirable because one considers it a good, can one believe that he who possesses the sovereign good is without reward? Remember that since the good is beatitude <term>beatitude: a state of supreme blessedness or perfect happiness</term>, he who is good is blessed; but with what happiness? With that which makes him God <note>original: "Dieu". In this Neoplatonic context, Lady Philosophy argues that because God is happiness itself, those who attain happiness through virtue participate in the divine nature, essentially becoming "gods" by participation.</note>. The prize of virtue is thus of such a nature that the ages do not consume it, an enemy power does nothing to diminish it, and malice does not alter it at all.

This being so, a Wise man <note>original: "vn Sage". This refers to the Stoic and Platonic ideal of a person who lives according to reason and virtue.</note> cannot ignore the punishments of those who are not wise, since good and evil being opposites, they must be opposed in their effects, which are rewards and punishments; and therefore, just as goodness is the prize of the Good, malice is the wages of the Wicked. And thus, if they wish to have reasonable thoughts about what they are, can they consider themselves exempt from punishment, since iniquity—which is the most severe of all torments—does not merely trouble them, but actually overwhelms them?

You can further recognize their misery by the happiness of the righteous. I said a little while ago that everything that is made is one, and everything that is one is good: from which one can draw by a necessary consequence that everything that is made is good. And thus, everything that degenerates from the good begins to cease to be; which clearly shows that the wicked are no longer what they were, and although their exterior still makes them appear as men, malice strips them of their nature. It will be easy for you to conceive of this if you consider that since virtue alone is capable of raising man above his human condition, if malice causes him to change his state, it is only to place him in one worse than that which is natural to him. It happens then that he who...

...vices have changed [him], he is no longer a man. A miser burns with the desire to seize the property of others; is he not a wolf? His mouth utters only words of quarrel; is the comparison to a dog not apt? <note>original: "naiue". In this 17th-century context, it means natural, plain, or fittingly described.</note> He flatters himself for having craftily deceived; do foxes not do the same? Does anger rob him of speech? Does a lion have more cruelty? Fear makes him dread even the safest things; does he not have the courage of a deer? Laziness makes him languish; does he lead any other life than that of the ass? Does fickleness change his plans from moment to moment? Is he any different from the birds? Sensuality <term>sensuality: "la volupté," referring here specifically to base physical pleasures that override reason</term> keeps him always in the mud; do pigs do any more? And that is how he who scorns being a man, unable to reach the condition of the Gods, is lowered to that of wild beasts.
