### IV. PROSE.

It is without injustice that one can believe that the wicked are like savage beasts; though they appear as men in their faces, they are brutes in their behavior. But it would be desirable that while malice gave them the cruelty of animals, it had also left them the inability to harm good people. P. <note>The speaker "P." refers to Lady Philosophy, the personified guide who consoles Boethius.</note> Also, they do not have that power, as I will show elsewhere; and if one had taken away from the wicked that which seems to give them the freedom to do evil, their punishments would be relieved by more than half. For it is certain (though common sense has difficulty agreeing with this truth) that they are more miserable through the success of their evil desires than through the inability to carry them out. Because if it is a great misery to desire an evil, it is an extreme one to be able to do it, since without the power, an evil would be nothing but a bad thought. If your judgment fixes upon the good, do not expect your reward from the sentence of a Judge; the choice you have made for the most equitable side serves as your recompense. If you do the contrary, seek no other vengeance than your own error; you condemn yourself to misery. It is the same as if you withdraw your sight from Heaven to fix it upon the earth: at one moment your thought will be among the Stars, and now in the mud. 

The common people do not make such fine reflections; must we nevertheless join our judgment to the brutal passions of those who ought only to pass for beasts? <header>The Consolation</header>
<sig>POE</sig>

...able? <note>Continuing from the previous page: "those [miser]able?"</note> Then it must be admitted that those who are deserving of some punishment are unhappy. Now tell me: if you were a Judge, would you order punishment for the one who committed the offense, or rather for the one who suffered the injury? Without a doubt, you would seek satisfaction for the outrage in the suffering of the one who committed it. Therefore, in your opinion, the one who commits an injury is more miserable than the one who receives it; and injustice is the misfortune of the doer, and not of the sufferer. It is true that **Orators** <note>In this context, public speakers or lawyers who plead cases in court.</note> try to incite pity in the Judges by recounting the outrages suffered by the innocent, although those who cause the harm are more worthy of compassion than those who have endured the inconveniences. And certainly, **Accusers** should only bring criminals before Judges as though they were sick patients, who must be cured through punishments; thus, their very accusation would serve as their defense. Truly, if the **wicked** still had enough light <note>Referring to moral or spiritual clarity.</note> to perceive virtue, they would see that the only way to erase the ugliness of vice would be to receive its punishment, which they would take care not to consider a misery. And so, the defense of a lawyer would offend them; they would surrender themselves to the accusers, and the only favor they would expect from the Judges would be the severity of their **sentences**. <note>original: "arrests," referring to judicial decrees or legal judgments.</note> From this, you may learn that the Sages feel no hatred, for who could hate the good unless they declared themselves insane? As for the wicked, I do not see that the aversion one feels toward them is reasonable, since malice is a sickness of the mind, just as a lingering illness is an infirmity of the body. A man of judgment never becomes angry at a fever, but rather tries to cure it; likewise, one ought to have compassion for the wicked, and not be so provoked by their faults.

<summary>Philosophy argues that the perpetrator of an injustice is more pitiable than the victim, as vice is a "sickness of the mind" that should be treated with the "medicine" of punishment. She concludes that wise people should feel compassion rather than hatred for the wicked, viewing their crimes as a spiritual infirmity.</summary>

<header>The Consolation</header>

...ills. <note>This word completes the sentence from the previous page: "...Fortune has some goods mixed with its ills."</note> And in truth, I cannot persuade myself that there is any wise man so ill-advised as to prefer being exiled, poor, and burdened with contempt, than to have great riches, to be powerful, and honored in his own country; since it is certain that a prosperous wisdom <note>original: "heureuſe ſageſſe," implying wisdom accompanied by good fortune.</note> is more useful, and is better transmitted to those who live under its guidance, than a virtue which is weak and destitute. And furthermore, were prisons not built for crimes? Have laws and punishments not been ordained against the wicked? Yet vice steals the reward of virtues, and virtue suffers the punishments of vice. To tell the truth, I cannot marvel enough at seeing such an irrational reversal; being ignorant of the cause, I would desire to learn it from you. My astonishment would be less if I could persuade myself that chance governed the world: but since this God—who does good to the good and evil to the wicked, and most often evil to the good and good to the wicked—is the one who leads it, my mind cannot find the difference between His providence and the random encounters of Fortune.

<note>The following "B." indicates the response of Philosophy.</note>

**B.** It is no wonder, being ignorant of the order of the world, that you deem it confused: nevertheless, you must command your mind to believe that, the governor of the world being good, its conduct cannot be evil.

## VI. PROSE.

It is so, nonetheless; since it is for you to reveal the reasons hidden from us, I desire that you declare to me the causes of this great miracle. <note>The "miracle" refers to the confusing distribution of good and bad fortune in the world, which the narrator finds inexplicable.</note> You are engaging me in no small undertaking (Wisdom <note>Lady Philosophy</note> replied with a smile); the struggle of Hercules against the Hydra was no more difficult. <note>A reference to the second labor of Hercules; as soon as he cut off one head of the Hydra, others grew back. Philosophy uses this to describe how one philosophical question leads to many others.</note> Hardly will I have satisfied one difficulty before the same subject will present us with a great number of questions that it will be impossible to resolve except with the help of a strong and powerful genius. It is a matter here of Providence, of order, of chance, of unforeseen events, of knowledge, of divine predestination, and of the freedom of our will. You see the importance of this discourse. Although time presses us, I nevertheless wish to reveal some truths to you, since knowing them forms a part of your healing. But if the sweetness of a musical melody charms you, you must delay that pleasure a little to make yourself attentive to the melody of reason. Everything that receives life through birth, and through the succession of generations, and everything that has movement, derives its causes, its order, and its regulation from the constant firmness of God. It is from his simplicity, gathered within himself, that all things take their motion; <note>original: *bransle*. --- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse from the previous section where Lady Philosophy began explaining the complex relationship between Providence, Fate, and human will. She is responding to the narrator's confusion about why good and bad things happen seemingly at random.</meta>

<header>of Philosophy. Book IV.</header>

...ly Destiny. <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the nature of God's plan.</note> These two things will appear different to all those who understand their nature: in that Providence in God is nothing other than the divine reason that guides all creatures; Fate is the arrangement that Providence imposes upon the order of their actions. Providence embraces all things, however different and infinite they may be; but Fate marks the particular movements of beings, arranges them in their rank, and gives them their form and duration. Thus, this arrangement, when related to the knowledge of God, is simply what we call Providence; but when considered in the passage of time and in the sequence that things have among themselves, we call it Fate. Although these two things are different, one depends on the other, because the order of Fate is an effect of Providence.

For just as an architect, conceiving the idea of a work, creates it in a certain way all at once in his mind, and afterwards works it out in the execution; in the same way, God takes the designs of everything through Providence, and handles them externally through Fate. Whether Fate receives its movements from some Divinity, or takes its impressions from the soul, or from all of nature, from the power of Angels, and the artifice of demons, or from the influence of the Stars; whether all these things concur in this order, it is evident that the simple and constant idea of everything to be done is nothing but the Providence of God, and that Fate is like the hand of this Providence, which places feasible things in the successive sequence of time; or rather, it is like the ever-tightening knot of creatures. <note>The "ever-tightening knot" refers to the way all created beings are interconnected and bound by the laws of cause and effect over time.</note> From this, it follows that nothing is exempt from Providence, not even Fate, which does not extend to everything that Providence con-

I
<meta>catchword: con- [continues as "contains" or "considers" on the following page]</meta>

<summary>Lady Philosophy distinguishes between Providence—the timeless, divine blueprint in God's mind—and Fate—the unfolding of that plan within the constraints of time and physical reality. She explains that while they are distinct, Fate is entirely dependent on and a tool of divine Providence.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, Fate, divine reason, architect analogy, order of nature, time vs eternity, angels and demons, celestial influence</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues Lady Philosophy’s explanation of the relationship between divine Providence (God’s timeless plan) and Fate (the execution of that plan in the physical world). She uses a geometric analogy of concentric circles to explain how closeness to God grants stability.</meta>

### The Consolation

...led, <note>This completes the word "conducted" or "led" from the previous page.</note> especially as there are things which, because they are joined to the **unchanging essence of God**, are above the shifts of **Fate**. This comparison will make my thought intelligible to you. Imagine several globes turning on the same pivot; the one that approaches closest to the middle <unclear>is</unclear> like the center of all the others, around <unclear>which</unclear> they turn. The one, on the contrary, that has a larger circumference must make a greater detour. 

But if something joins itself to this center, it gathers and constrains itself without spreading outward. Thus, the further a thing separates itself from the primary **intelligence** <note>In this context, "Intelligence" refers to the Divine Mind or the pure reason of God.</note>, the more it is subject to **Fate**; and a thing is less subject to Fate's whims the more closely it is united to this foundation of all things; because the stability of this first Being raises it above the necessities of Fate. 

Therefore, the relationship is the same as:
- **Reasoning** is to the power of **discourse**; <note>Discourse here refers to the step-by-step process of human logic, whereas reasoning/intelligence refers to direct, immediate understanding.</note>
- That which is **created** is to that which **produces**;
- **Time** is to **eternity**;
- The **circle** is to its **center**.

The same relationship is found in the changes of **Fate** when compared to the simple being of **Providence**. It is this Fate that guides the **Stars** and moves the **Heavens**; it is Fate that binds the **Elements** and, through continual changes, makes them alter their appearance and nature. It is Fate that continues and preserves species through the sequence of generations and the production of seeds; it is Fate likewise that handles all the forms of men and brings order to their actions. These actions, taking their direction from this **Providence** which we have said to be unchanging, are by a necessary consequence free from changes. And thus all Beings are very well governed, provided they do not withdraw from this Providence, to which alone it belongs to set the order and the inviolable rank among the causes that maintain all

<meta>catchword: things</meta>

<summary>Lady Philosophy uses the metaphor of concentric circles to explain that the closer a soul or object is to the "center" (God/Providence), the less it is tossed about by the "circumference" (Fate/Time). She argues that while Fate governs the changing physical world, it is ultimately an instrument of God's stable plan.</summary>

### On Philosophy. Book IV.

<page-num>131</page-num>

...things by its own **immutability**. From this it follows that because your mind cannot grasp the connections of this order, you consider it full of confusion, although there is nothing better regulated, and every creature tends toward the good by its own direction. The reason for this is that even the **wicked** seek evil only under the appearance of good; <note>Lady Philosophy argues that no one seeks evil for its own sake; rather, they are mistaken about what will truly bring them happiness.</note> and thus, if it happens that someone turns away from the right path, it is their error that deceives them, and not this order that causes them to fail. 

But what greater **disorder** (you will say to me) than to see the **Good** and the **Wicked** share equally in both blessings and misfortunes, and live sometimes in good fortune and sometimes suffering from bad? I was expecting this objection. What? Do the minds of men presume so much that they believe the good and the wicked should have no other fortune than that which seems equitable to them? 

The opinions of men do not agree on this point, since those who deserve rewards in the judgment of some are worthy of punishment in the opinion of others. Nevertheless, let us assume the case that distinguishing good people from those who are not is easy; perhaps one could then penetrate into the secret of their **character** <note>original: *genie*. In this context, it refers to the innate nature or natural disposition of an individual.</note>, which is like the **temperament** of the mind. 

It is no small feat of knowledge to understand why sweets are tasteless to some, while others find bitterness agreeable; why certain sick people are cured only by gentle remedies, while others can only be relieved by violent ones. The **Physician** to whom this knowledge belongs feels no wonder at these contradictions. Souls have no other health than **good morals**, nor any other diseases than **vices**; likewise there...

<summary>Lady Philosophy explains that human confusion regarding the "unfair" distribution of fortune stems from an inability to see the divine order. She uses a medical analogy, suggesting that God, like a physician, provides different "remedies" (fortunes) based on the specific needs and characters of different souls.</summary>

# Consolation

<page-num>132</page-num>

<meta>catchword: Providence</meta>

...is it but God alone who can preserve the former and heal the latter? For being as if in a <term>watchtower<note>original: "eschauguette." This refers to a small turret or lookout post on a castle wall, used here to describe God’s elevated perspective over all creation.</note></term> from where His providence discovers all the most secret needs of creatures, He relieves them, giving to each what is proper to it. And this is from where arises this miraculous interconnectedness of all things, which makes itself admired by those who are ignorant, and loved by those who recognize its true cause. And so that I may gather in a few words what our reason can understand of the profound knowledge of God: that which your error believes to be very just is not so in the estimation of this Providence which knows all. 

Has not our good friend <term>Lucan<note>A Roman poet (39–65 AD) known for his epic poem "Pharsalia," which details the civil war between Julius Caesar and Pompey.</note></term> left in writing that the Gods and <term>Cato<note>Cato the Younger, a Stoic philosopher and statesman who chose suicide over submission to Caesar's tyranny.</note></term> did not agree on the same side in the war of Pharsalia, since the Gods favored the side that Cato condemned? <note>Lady Philosophy alludes to the famous line: "The winning cause pleased the gods, but the losing cause pleased Cato." It suggests that human moral judgment and divine will do not always appear to align from our perspective.</note> You see then that everything done against your judgment does not cease to be the natural order of things, although in your thought it is but <unclear>confusion</unclear>. 

I wish, nonetheless, that there were a mind so well-formed that it had only judgments conforming to that of God. Truly, the virtue of men is so delicate that it is at risk of abandoning innocence if it cannot retain its fortune. Must not the goodness of God therefore accommodate the weaknesses of those whom adversity can change? Is there anyone so <unclear>perfect</unclear> that his virtue <unclear>allows</unclear> him to approach very near to God? His all-wise <unclear>Providence</unclear> does not even allow illnesses to touch him; for as someone who has nobler thoughts than I has said: *The virtues compose the body of a holy man and make up its parts.* <note>This is likely a reference to Neoplatonic thought, suggesting that a truly virtuous person becomes a unified whole through their excellence.</note> From this it very often happens that the entire conduct of affairs is deferred to good people, so that the malice of the wicked may be repressed. # of Philosophy. Book IV.

<page-num>133</page-num>

<sig>I 3</sig>

Providence <note>original: "Prouidẽce." In the original French, the tilde over the 'e' indicates a missing 'n', a common shorthand in early printing.</note> mingles goods and evils for some; she allows others to be troubled, so that their patience may be strengthened by the experience of bitter things, and in misfortunes, for fear that a too-soft prosperity should corrupt them. There are those who fear without judgment what they could support without difficulty; there are others who despise too thoughtlessly what they cannot sustain, and it is to these that God makes them recognize their rashness through the impatience of their miseries. Here are some who are quite the opposite of those: we have seen men who have acquired a beautiful memory in the remembrance of posterity by a noble <note>original: "genereuse." In this context, it reflects the Latin *generosus*, meaning noble-spirited or high-born in character, specifically referring to a courageous death like that of a martyr or hero.</note> death. Some have left fine examples, and shown that pain could not conquer virtue. There is no reason to doubt that all this succeeds to the advantage of those who undertake it, and even the wicked receive their good and bad Fortune only in this manner. As for evils, no one will find that they suffer them unjustly, since their pains are their punishments, and our instructions. If at times they taste of what is good, the virtuous can take from that an excellent proof of the greatness of happiness <note>original: "felicité." Philosophy argues that if even the wicked can be wealthy or successful, then worldly success cannot be the highest good.</note>, since it allows itself to be possessed even by criminals. I note again a great sweetness in the conduct of this Providence: namely, that to withdraw from vice one whom the necessity of riches <note>The text suggests that some people commit crimes out of a desperate need for money; by giving them wealth, Providence removes the "necessity" or excuse for their vice.</note> might lead there, she gives him abundance, from which it happens that considering his vices and comforts, he corrects the former for fear of losing the latter; and therefore he will change his bad habits <note>original: "mœurs." This refers to one's moral character, customs, or way of life.</note>, and to always enjoy his goods, he will leave his crimes. Sometimes a too-great happiness justly ruins those who possess it. From this, Providence shows us this great miracle: that the wicked become good through their hatred of the vicious, so as to avoid being like those whom they cannot love. It belongs only to the power of God to make good use of evil and to change it into virtue. Order governs all; so that if something withdraws from His guidance, it is only to return to it in another way, so that chance usurps nothing within the domain of Providence. It is difficult to understand all the holy devices <note>original: "artifices." In the 17th century, this term referred to skillful craftsmanship or the intricate workings of a master builder, rather than deception.</note> of God, and certainly man is capable of neither conceiving nor explaining all the inner workings of this divine Wisdom. Our curiosity must be content to know that God guides all things toward the good, and that the beautiful order He has placed in the world banishes evil from it. And although our thoughts find much of it there, if we look upon this Providence, we shall condemn those thoughts. But I perceive that you are beginning to grow weary of this overly long speculation, and that the weight of this difficult reasoning makes you desire the sweetness of some verses; receive these to prepare you for the discourse that follows.

*And the firm support of his hand,*
*One would not see tomorrow again*
*Through this admirable intelligence,*
*It is upon him that movement*
*Has its secure foundation.*
*We have no other guidance,*
*Than the holy laws of this God;*
*His lovely breast is the place,*
*Where our journey ends:*
*This sweet love, which maintains us,*
*Seeks the source from which it comes.*

**B.** To tell the truth, your reasoning is so well supported that it cannot be contradicted, and if I turn my thoughts to this <term>Providence</term> <note>The divine guidance or care of God over the universe.</note> and to this destiny you have declared to me, I cannot ignore the cause of its firmness. Yet it must be admitted that these truths are not in accordance with common sense, insofar as the ordinary opinion of men is that there is such a thing as "bad" Fortune.

**P.** I am happy to accommodate their temperament, lest my Philosophy seem either cruel or <unclear>inhumane</unclear> <note>This word is partially obscured by a large ink blot in the original manuscript.</note>. Do you not grant me that everything useful is good? You cannot deny it; that which corrects vices or increases virtues is profitable, and therefore it cannot be bad. Certainly, the Fortune of those who seek the innocent pleasures of virtue, and who try to find the right path, having these qualities, cannot be esteemed troublesome by the People. Will it not be the condition of the wicked that one believes to be miserable, since its purpose is to <term>chastise</term> <note>To discipline or punish with the intent of improvement.</note> the evil-doers? Take care not to have such bad thoughts; we have shown clearly that the fortune of those who are in the pursuit or the enjoyment of virtue is always good, and that of the wicked can only be bad if it continues in injustice. 

And therefore a wise man should find it no more strange to see himself troubled by adversity than a brave man to hear the cries and the noise of an army: especially since the difficulty of suffering serves as material for the glory of the one, and for the wisdom of the other. And in fact, <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "vertu" — Philosophy here plays on the etymological link between the Latin *virtus* (virtue) and *vir* (man/courage/strength).</note> takes its name only from courage, by which it overcomes all troublesome things; because the intention of those who value virtue is not to soften themselves in delights.

<summary>Lady Philosophy argues that all Fortune is technically "good" because it either rewards the virtuous, tests the brave, or corrects the wicked. And therefore, so that the caresses of a good fortune do not corrupt you, or the inconveniences of a bad one do not overturn you; hold yourselves firm in the middle, because everything at the extremes has only the appearance of happiness, and not the reward of labor. It is in your power to shape your own fortunes, since even that which seems disagreeable exercises virtue, and corrects or punishes vice.

has been debated by anyone: that nothing

something were born from nothing, it would have
no cause. Since that is impossible, I conclude
reasonably that there is no such thing as chance. What, is

Beings to whom these names are appropriate? Aristotle has

<summary>Philosophy and Boethius begin Book V by debating the existence of chance. Philosophy argues that in a universe ordered by God, "chance" is a meaningless term because nothing can happen without a cause.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, chance, Aristotle, Physics, causality, Boethius, creation from nothing</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 140 where Philosophy defines chance. It concludes the prose explanation of chance as a meeting of unforeseen causes and moves into the first poetic meter of Book V, which uses the metaphor of the Tigris and Euphrates rivers to illustrate divine order behind apparent randomness.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
-># of Philosophy. Book V.<-

one end, and another results contrary to the intention of the person who acts, we call this chance <note>Original: *hazard*</note>, as if someone worked in his field with the intention of sowing it, and found a treasure; that is what we call fortuitous <note>Original: *fortuit*</note>. This, however, is not made from nothing, insofar as it has causes which, though they are unknown to us, nonetheless remain true. And truly, if the laborer had not worked in his field, and if the miser <note>Original: *l'Auare*</note> had not hidden his treasure there, it would not have been found. Chance, then, is nothing other than that which occurs through the meeting of several causes that act without the design of the worker. And certainly, he who had hidden his money did not intend for it to be found, nor did he who plowed his land have its discovery as his goal. Chance is therefore the effect of two causes which concur in an action, not in a tumultuous way, but through a secret guidance of that wise Providence which has established the beautiful order that we admire in Nature.

### I. POETRY.

*The Tigris mingles in the waters of the Euphrates,*
*Where the soldier flees even as he wishes to overcome,*
*And then it spreads out*
*Returning to the bed it had just left.*
*If their waters later form a new alliance,*
*The burdens they carried take the same detours,*
*And the boat moves forward*
*In the order and design that God places in their course.*
*In the same way, the chance that shapes our adventures,*
*Though we judge it to be conducted without laws,*
*Has, within these proceedings,*
*The secret of the designs of the Monarch of Kings.*

## II. PROSE.

I begin to understand that what you say is true, but I beg you to teach me if, in this chain of causes, man preserves his free agency <note>Original: *franchise de sa liberté*</note>, or if the movements of his will remain constrained under this fatal chain.

P. A rational creature is always free, and God has given him the use of free will <term>free will: the power of acting without the constraint of necessity or fate; in French, "franc arbitre"</term> only to make him recognize what should be either desired or avoided. His will chooses what is desirable and turns away from what is not; and thus those who possess reason <note>Original: *discours*, referring to the faculty of rational thought</note> have the power of choice. But this power of willing is not in all creatures. In those essences that are entirely pure and celestial <note>Referring to angels or divine intelligences</note>, which have no alliance with matter, judgment is enlightened, the will is incorruptible, and the power to fulfill their desires is inviolable. As for rational souls, it is impossible to take away this freedom, particularly when they keep themselves in the contemplation of the first and supreme Being. They are less free when they lower themselves to sensible things <note>Sensible things: objects perceived by the physical senses rather than understood by the intellect</note>, and even less so when they are joined with bodies; but in truth, they are entirely enslaved when they obey their evil inclinations and abandon the rule of reason. For no sooner have they turned their eyes away from the true and supreme light than they are immediately blinded. And thus their own will causes their servitude, and their freedom is only "free" in the sense that it chooses to be a slave. God, who sees everything from eternity, knows this through the vision of His Providence, and arranges it according to the designs of His divine counsel: *He sees all, He hears all.* <note>A reference to a line from Homer used to illustrate divine omniscience</note>

<vocab>Philosophy, free will, liberty, Providence, reason, soul, Boethius</vocab>

<summary>Philosophy explains that while all rational beings possess free will, its strength varies: it is absolute in divine beings but becomes diminished in humans when they prioritize physical desires over rational contemplation.</summary>
<keywords>free will, rational soul, divine providence, slavery to vice, celestial essences, Boethius, omniscience</keywords>

## II. POETRY.

*Homer names the Sun,* <note>The Greek poet Homer often refers to Helios (the Sun) as "all-seeing" because it illuminates the world during the day.</note>
*The Creator of light,*
*The all-seeing, the peerless one,*
*Though he does not open his eyelid,*
*Neither under the Earth, nor under the Water,*
*Where the fires of his torch were never seen.*
*But the great God of the Universe,*
*Before whom everything is made of glass,* <note>A metaphor for total transparency; nothing can be hidden from the divine gaze.</note>
*Casts His gaze through*
*The solid body of the Earth,*
*Night does not veil His eyes,*
*Though with its darkness, it eclipses the Heavens.*
*What was, is, and what shall be,*
*Is present to His knowledge,*
*And nothing will ever limit*
*His eternal intelligence,*
*His eye passes from one end to the other,*
*It is thus the true Sun which shines and sees all.*

## III. PROSE.

I feel myself entangled in new difficulties, and it is difficult for me to understand the harmony between God’s foreknowledge <term>foreknowledge: "prescience" in the original French; the theological concept of God knowing the future before it happens</term> and our freedom. For if it is true that His Providence sees things in the future, and that it cannot be deceived, it seems that this foreknowledge makes those things necessary. And so, God having seen from all eternity not only the actions of men, but also their counsels <note>Original: *conseils*, referring here to their inner deliberations and intentions</note> and the most secret wills of their hearts, I can recognize no freedom: since this knowledge, which cannot be de-

<meta>catchword: ceived,</meta>

<summary>This page contrasts the limited "all-seeing" nature of the physical sun with the absolute omniscience of God and introduces the philosophical problem of how human free will can exist if God already knows every future action.</summary>
<keywords>omniscience, divine sight, foreknowledge, free will, providence, Homer, Boethius</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical argument presented by the prisoner Boethius regarding the conflict between divine foreknowledge and human free will. In the previous section, Boethius argued that if God sees all things in advance, human actions must happen by necessity. He now refines this argument, rejecting a common theological shortcut that suggests God's knowledge doesn't "cause" the event.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>The Consolation</header>

...deceived, <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page: "even if our opinion is deceived, He [God] has foreseen them."</note> He has foreseen them. Otherwise, if the outcome could be changed, it would no longer be a sure foreknowledge <term>foreknowledge: "prescience" in the original French; the ability to know events before they occur</term> of future things, but an uncertain conjecture <note>Original: *coniecture*; implying guesswork rather than divine certainty.</note> of possible events, which is a thought one cannot have of God. For my part, I cannot accept an answer by which people try to untangle themselves from this difficulty, when they say that nothing happens simply *because* God foresees it, but rather that He sees it because He cannot be ignorant of anything; and thus, if there is a necessity, it regards the knowledge and not its object, because it is not necessary that a foreseen thing happens, but it is not a matter of freedom that what must happen is not foreseen. 

As if we were struggling to know whether foreknowledge is the cause of the necessity of future things, or if the necessity of future things is the cause of foreknowledge. For my part, I want to show that—whatever the order of causes may be—the necessity of foreseen events remains, even if this foreknowledge seems to leave them a total freedom. 

I will use a fairly familiar comparison. If someone is sitting, the judgment formed about this state of rest is necessarily true; and reciprocally, if this belief is true, it is also necessary that he be sitting. There is therefore necessity in both: in the one, the necessity of being seated, and in the other, the necessity of being true. However, it is not true that he is sitting simply because one believes it; rather, one believes it because he is truly sitting. And so, although the one is the particular cause of the truth of the other, it seems nonetheless that there is a common necessity between these two things. 

The same reasoning can be applied to Providence <note>Divine guidance or the care of God over the creatures of the earth.</note> and future things. For even though the event is the cause of the foreknowledge, and not the foreknowledge the cause of the event, it is nonetheless necessary that these future things be foreseen by God, and that they happen as

<summary>Boethius examines the logical link between God's certain knowledge and the necessity of events, using the analogy of a person sitting to show that truth and reality are bound together by necessity.</summary>
<keywords>divine foreknowledge, free will, necessity, Boethius, Providence, logic, truth</keywords>

they were foreseen, which seems sufficient for the ruin of liberty <note>The "ruin of liberty" refers to the destruction of free will; if an action is known by God before it happens, the prisoner argues it must happen, leaving no room for a different choice.</note>. Let us now see how unreasonable it is to say that the outcome of things that take place in time is the cause of God's eternal knowledge. And, I ask you, to wish that God foresees the future because it must happen—is that not the same as believing that past things are the cause of this sovereign Providence? But just as it is necessary that a thing exists when I know that it exists, so too when I foresee a thing, it is not free for it not to be future; from which I conclude that the occurrence of a foreseen thing is not avoidable. Furthermore, if someone forms an opinion about anything other than how it is, this is not certain knowledge, but an uncertain belief, which is very far removed from the nature of science <term>science: from the Latin *scientia*, meaning certain, infallible knowledge rather than the modern experimental method</term>. Therefore, if a thing is future in such a way that its occurrence is not necessary, who could know that it must happen? For just as science is not mixed with falsehood or uncertainty, so too it cannot be otherwise than it is conceived. This is why science is without lie, since it is necessary that each thing be as science judges it to be. How then can it be that God foresees the future if it is uncertain? For if what He foresees must infallibly happen can [instead] not happen, He is mistaken—which cannot be said or even thought, unless one makes oneself guilty of blasphemy. But if He foresees only that it *might* be or *might* not be, what kind of knowledge is that, which has nothing certain or assured? In what way is it different from that ridiculous oracle of Tiresias <note>Tiresias: a blind prophet of Greek myth. This specific "ridiculous" quote—*Everything I say will either be or will not be*—is a reference to Horace’s *Satires* (II.5.59), mocking prophecies that are so vague they are always technically "true."</note>: *Everything I say will either be or will not be.* What advantage would this foreknowledge have over the opinion of men, if He judged the event—

<meta>This page continues the narrator’s (Boethius) logical struggle with the concept of divine foreknowledge. On the previous page, he argued that if God's knowledge is certain, then the future must be fixed; here, he explores the devastating moral and spiritual consequences of such a deterministic worldview.</meta>

...enly judging uncertain things, as they do? For if there can be no doubt in the knowledge of God, it must be admitted that the outcome of what He foresees is necessary. And therefore, there is no liberty in the plans and actions of man, which God has thus bound to the necessity of the outcome. 

If we once accept this thought—(Philosophy replied) <note>Though the text attributes this to Philosophy, the narrator is still articulating the "dangerous consequences" of the logic he has just built, leading up to Philosophy’s eventual rebuttal.</note> as it seems this discourse must lead us to—what disorder do we bring into our conduct? In vain will punishments and rewards be decreed for actions in which the will plays no part; thus, the impunity of the wicked and the reward of the good, which seems unjust to us, would seem quite fair, because necessity would have forced the actions of the wicked by the infallible necessity of the result. Therefore, we should no longer seek a distinction between vices and virtues. 

From these dangerous principles would arise this evil consequence: that since the order of all things comes from the Providence of God, without the wisdom of men contributing anything of its own, all our sins and all our evils must be attributed to the Author of all good. Thus, there is no longer any need for hope, nor for offering prayers; for I ask you, what need is there to desire or ask for that which is necessary in its occurrence? This entirely destroys the communion <note>original: "commerce," referring to the spiritual exchange or relationship</note> of prayers and desires between God and men, since it is only the humility of our prayers <note>original: "vœux," meaning vows or heartfelt petitions</note> that makes us worthy of His grace and brings us close to that inaccessible light. And so we must concede what I recognized a moment ago: that man, separated from his source <term>principle: the "principe" or origin from which a being derives its existence and purpose</term>, falls back into his nothingness.

Which made of them a holy alliance,
    And which maintained them without contradictions?
    Perhaps the truth never quarrels,
    And its friendship is forever faithful,
        But our reason,
Unable to penetrate the tight knot which binds it
To other truths, a foolish madness
Seeks to persuade it that it is without connection.
    Whence comes it then that the spirit does such violence,
To probe objects, and draw forth knowledge
    Of what is hidden?
Does it know the secrets it tries to know?
If it knows them not, does it dare to appear,
And is it not ashamed to see itself bound to them?
    Who was ever enamored of an unknown thing?
Who could seek it, having never seen it?
    And when a happy fate
Might place before the spirit the sought-after form;
If it does not know it, it is still hidden,
And therefore all its senses make but a vain effort. <note>This stanza references "Meno's Paradox" from Plato: if you don't know what you're looking for, how will you know when you've found it?</note>

### IV. PROSE.

Behold (continues Wisdom) the ancient problem <note>original: "la vieilleſſe," literally "the old age," referring here to a long-standing philosophical debate.</note> that so troubled the mind of Cicero in his books concerning Prophecies, <note>A reference to Cicero's "De Divinatione" (On Divination), where he discusses whether the ability to predict the future negates free will.</note> and which you now consider so curiously. Nevertheless, no one has yet truly untied its knot. The cause of this ignorance stems from the fact that the discourse of human reason is not capable of reaching that simple foreknowledge of God; if it were possible to conceive of it, there would no longer be any reason to doubt. I shall attempt to clear away these clouds of ignorance as soon as I have untangled the difficulties that trouble you.

I would first like to know why you refuse to accept the answer of those who maintain that liberty is not constrained by the foreknowledge of things, simply because such knowledge does not make them necessary. Could you deduce the necessity of future things from any other source, if not for the fact that foreseen events cannot fail to happen? If it is true that foresight brings no necessity to the event (as you have acknowledged yourself), why would you bind free actions to the necessity of an outcome? To recognize this, let us imagine there were no foreknowledge; undoubtedly, free actions would not derive necessity from something that did not exist. Let us now suppose that this knowledge regards them, but imposes no necessity upon them; the will...

<meta>This page continues Prose 4 of Book V. Lady Philosophy is responding to Boethius's argument that if God knows the future, then the future must be fixed and human free will must be an illusion. She is currently addressing the idea that foreknowledge acts as a "sign" of necessity.</meta>

will remain entirely free. It is true (you will tell me) that the knowledge of what must happen does not make it necessary, but it is a sign that it is so; and thus, even if there were no anticipated knowledge, it would nevertheless be certain that the outcome of future things would not be free, insofar as a sign only marks what exists and does not cause it. This is why it must first be shown that nothing happens without necessity, in order to say that this Foreknowledge <note>original: "Preſcience." From the Latin *praescientia*, meaning "knowing beforehand."</note> is the sign of it. Otherwise, if there is no necessity, there can be no mark of it. Now, it is evident that the proof of this necessity must not be taken from the sign—from what is external to things—but indeed from their nature.

But how can it be that what Providence <note>original: "Prouidence." Divine guidance or care; here, God's ability to see and provide for the future.</note> foresees as having to happen, does not happen? Is there not a likelihood that we doubt the outcome of these things that foreknowledge foresees? Why shall we not rather believe that, though they happen, they have no necessity in their nature? Let this thought facilitate your understanding of this. 

We see quite commonly the skill <note>original: "l’addreſſe," meaning dexterity or cleverness.</note> that coachmen <note>original: "Carroſſiers." In the 17th century, this referred to drivers of horse-drawn carriages or chariots.</note> bring to driving their chariots (as we might say of other things); perhaps our eyes render their movements necessary because we see them? That cannot occur to a reasonable mind, being so far from the truth. And in fact, if these movements were necessary, I do not see why Art <note>original: "l’Art," referring here to human craft, technique, or practiced skill.</note> would bring so much care to effects that were constrained and forced. Therefore, that which is free when it is done is not necessary when it is foreseen. And thus it is with things that must happen, whose outcome nevertheless has no necessity. I do not believe anyone can be found who could say that what is being done at this hour was not once "the future."

this is not true, since we owe it principally

The eye, however distant it may be, sees it by the favor of its

rather in the knowledge of what they 
comprehend than in the power of the things 
that are conceived? And to tell the truth, this 
seems reasonable. For if judgment is in the act 
of the one who knows, it is absolutely 
necessary that each one accomplishes his action 
by his own particular strengths, and not by 
those which are foreign to him.

## V. PROSE.

If the mind uses only its own powers to understand physical bodies, even though certain invisible qualities have come before and, in a sense, awakened its activity: how much more reasonably should we say that an intelligence completely separated from any commerce with matter does not rely on sensory images <note>original: "especes sensibles." In medieval and early modern philosophy, "species" were the forms or mental "likenesses" of objects that traveled from the object to the senses.</note> to know them? Thus we see that nature has given various kinds of knowledge to different creatures. Shellfish and those fish which are as immobile as the rocks to which they are attached possess only sensation <note>original: "sentiment." Here meaning basic tactile perception or the ability to feel physical stimuli.</note>. Animals that seem to have desires and aversions are provided with imagination. Reasoned discourse belongs only to human nature, just as intelligence is the proper attribute of the divine; however, this latter faculty possesses all the perfections of the others. What would happen if the senses and the imagination were to contradict reason in the knowledge of universal and abstract things, simply because their own object of focus is not of that nature? Perhaps one might judge the reasoning to be false for conceiving that which is sensory and particular as a universal thing. Would reason not then be acting reasonably if it replied that it sees the sensory world and what can be imagined through a more noble and elevated knowledge? While it is impossible for the lower faculties to move beyond material images and forms, one must not judge the powers of the mind by the weaknesses of the body. And we, who are endowed with all these powers, should take the side of reason rather than the senses. This is the ju- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; it likely continues as "jugement" (judgment) on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues Lady Philosophy’s argument regarding the hierarchy of understanding. She concludes that while human reason is limited to seeing things in time, Divine Intelligence sees all things in an eternal "now." <page-num>154</page-num>
<header>The Consolation</header>

judgment that our limited reason makes of this foreknowledge <note>original: "prescience." The ability to know things before they happen.</note> concerning the future; for, since reason sees nothing beyond the present, it believes the same of Divine Intelligence. This is your reasoning: If a thing is necessary in its outcome, it can be foreseen with certainty; therefore, there is either no foreknowledge, or if we accept its existence, it is impossible to reject the necessity of the outcome of all things. Now, if we were capable of this high intelligence, just as we are capable of reasoned discourse, we would undoubtedly—just as we judge it fair that sense and imagination yield to reason—likewise submit all our reason to the Divine. And so, let us strive to raise our thoughts to that sovereign Intelligence; there, our reason will see truths that our own light does not discover. It is this: that which does not have a necessary outcome is nonetheless the object of a knowledge that cannot fail; and this divine sight is not an opinion, but a simple and completely perfect science <note>In the 17th-century context, "science" refers to "scientia"—certain, demonstrated knowledge, rather than the modern experimental method.</note>.

# VI. PROSE.

Since we have proved that everything which is known is known through the natural faculty of those who perceive it, and not by any power inherent in the objects of knowledge, let us strive (as much as our weakness allows) to understand the divine nature, so that this knowledge may lead us to the manner in which God knows all things. All who have held rational thoughts of God say that He is eternal. Let us enter into the consideration of this eternity; through it, we shall know His essence and His knowledge. Eternity is the perfect and complete possession <note>original: "iouïssance." In this context, it refers to the full attainment or enjoyment of a state of being.</note> of a life that is whole and simultaneous, without end, without beginning, and without division: this shall be made clear by comparison with time; for as much as everything that lives in

<note>The vocabulary below highlights the central themes of this concluding argument.</note>
**Philosophy, Eternity, God, Boethius, Knowledge, Divine Nature**

<summary>This page concludes a poem on the spiritual significance of man's upright posture and begins the final prose section defining eternity as a "simultaneous whole" beyond time. This definition is crucial for Boethius’s argument that God sees all events—past, present, and future—in a single eternal "now."</summary>

<keywords>Eternity, Divine Nature, Human Stature, Boethius, Philosophy, Knowledge, Time vs Eternity</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of eternity from the previous page, where Lady Philosophy explains the nature of God's knowledge. It specifically contrasts the "eternal" nature of God (existing outside of time) with the "perpetual" nature of the world as described by ancient philosophers like Aristotle and Plato.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>The Consolation</header>

its extent goes from the past, through the present, to the future. There is nothing that exists within this flux and succession that possesses its life all at once; rather, it awaits tomorrow while letting yesterday <note>original: "sa veille." Literally "its eve" or the night before.</note> slip away. And even of the present day, you hold only a single moment. Therefore, that which is subject to the passage of time—even if it has neither beginning nor end, as Aristotle <term>Aristotle</term> <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE) argued in his *Physics* and *De Caelo* that the universe had no beginning and would have no end, a view that challenged later Christian doctrines of creation.</note> believed of the world, and even if its duration extends to an infinity of ages—nevertheless, one cannot say that it is eternal. This is because its duration is not gathered and collected into a single point; and it does not possess the future as if it were present. 

That which fully enjoys its Being; to which nothing of the future is lacking; and from which the past does not escape, is, properly speaking, eternal. It is necessary that nothing outside itself be missing from it, and that it holds all the moments of the ages present at once. From this, it is easy to conclude that those are mistaken who believe, with Plato <term>Plato</term> <note>Referring to the *Timaeus*, where Plato describes time as a "moving image of eternity." The author here clarifies the distinction between "forever" (perpetual) and "outside of time" (eternal).</note>, that the world has no beginning or end, and consequently that it is of the same age as God and is co-eternal with Him. 

There is a great difference between possessing a life that has no boundaries (which Plato grants to the world) and having one whose duration is entirely present. That which is unique to God should not seem older to us than created things merely by the number of years, but by the inherent qualities of His simplest Being. For the sequence of time merely imitates the state of this unchanging and fully present life; and, unable to match it, time degenerates from stillness into movement, and from the simplicity of a life entirely present into the flowing away of an age that is always escaping. And since time cannot enjoy its life in its entirety because it never ends, it seems to imitate through its cycles that which it cannot possess all at once.

<summary>Philosophy distinguishes between the 'perpetual' existence of the universe (which flows through time) and the 'eternal' existence of God (which exists entirely in an unchanging present). She argues that God’s superiority is not based on the length of time He has existed, but on His existence outside of time itself.</summary>

And this is done by attaching itself to fleeing instants that never rest in a fixed point. Thus, time is an image of eternity: but as this life never stops, it spreads out toward the infinity of time, and thus it happens that it continues by flowing through what it could not possess by subsisting <note>The author distinguishes between "subsisting" (existing fully all at once, which is God's state) and "flowing" (existing piece by piece through time).</note>. And to tell the truth, if we wish to name things correctly, we shall say with Plato <term>Plato</term> <note>Plato (c. 427–347 BCE). The text refers to his *Timaeus*, where he describes time as the "moving image of eternity."</note> that God is eternal, and the world is perpetual. Therefore, since the way of conceiving follows the conditions of the Being in which it is found—God being eternal, simple, without vicissitude or change—His knowledge is likewise; so that without being subject to succession, it gathers the past, the present, and the future into this simple and eternal moment which presents everything to Him. 

And consequently, if we wish to consider foreknowledge <term>Prescience</term> <note>From the Latin *praescientia*, meaning "knowledge before." Philosophy argues here that God doesn't actually see things "before" they happen, but rather sees them in a "now" that encompasses all time.</note>, we shall not call it a prevision of the future, but rather a simple view of that which is always present. From which we can gather that the name "fore-sight" is less proper for it than that of "providence," insofar as the first suggests a future relation, and the second marks only the distance between the one who knows and the object which is known. And thus Providence is like a great eye placed upon the highest reaches of the Universe, which extends its gaze over all that is beneath it. 

What? Would you perhaps wish that God's knowledge should make an event necessary simply because He sees it? The knowledge of men does not have this imperfection. Tell me, I pray you, when you look at something, does it cease to be free? I could not bring myself to believe that you have such mistaken thoughts. If your eyes bring no necessity to what happens in time, say the same (for one may use a comparison) of those things which are considered...

...in eternity. This is why this divine vision alters nothing of the essence or the qualities of things <note>The original French uses "creatures," which in this context refers to all created things—human, animal, or inanimate—not just living beings.</note>, since God has them before Him just as they will be in the future. This is done without confusing or mixing the judgments He makes concerning both free actions and those that are not. Just as you, when seeing the Sun rolling through the heavens and a man walking upon the earth, judge the movement of the former to be necessary and the stroll of the latter to be free, without these two thoughts being confused. In the same way, the sight of God does not change the nature of things that are present to Him, even though, in relation to the distinction of times, they are future events. 

If you tell me that it is impossible for what God foresees not to happen, and thus that its outcome is necessary: I will confess a truth to you of which not all minds are capable, and which can only be understood by those whose deep speculation <term>Speculation</term> <note>From the Latin *speculatio*, meaning a "viewing" or "contemplation." It refers to an elevated form of reasoning that looks at things from God’s perspective.</note> penetrates into God. It is that future things are both necessary and free: necessary if they are considered in relation to this divine knowledge; free if they are taken in their own nature. This is easy enough to understand if you remember that there are two kinds of necessity: one absolute, the other by supposition <term>Supposition</term> <note>Also called "conditional necessity." For example, if you see someone sitting, they must necessarily be sitting at that moment; however, their act of sitting remains a free choice, not an absolute law of nature like gravity.</note>. That it is necessary for all men to die has no restriction; but that someone is walking, when you know it, cannot be otherwise—yet this circumstance cannot give the act a simple and absolute necessity, because it is not the nature of the action that carries it, but the meeting of the condition. The eye does not force the feet it sees moving freely to walk necessarily, even though it is not free for them not to move while their action lasts. Thus when God sees a thing as present, it

<summary>Philosophy explains the "Paradox of Foreknowledge" by distinguishing between absolute necessity (laws of nature) and conditional necessity (the fact that something is "necessary" only because it is currently happening and being observed).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical distinction between absolute and conditional necessity. It explains that while an event is "necessary" because God sees it happening in His eternal present, the event itself remains "free" in its own nature because its cause remains the human will.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>On Philosophy. Book V.</header>

it must necessarily be, although its being is not simply necessary <note>This refers to "conditional necessity." If you see a man walking, it is necessary that he is walking at that moment, but his nature does not require him to walk forever.</note>. Now it is certain that everything man is to do freely in the future is present to God. Therefore, future things are necessary because of this circumstance of God’s foresight, although in the properties of their natures, they remain fully free. All events that God foresees happen, therefore, necessarily, although before their existence, they might not happen. 

But what good does it do them to be of this nature, since the sole knowledge of God carries the weight of all the necessities one could imagine? Here it is: the movement of the Sun and that of a man are both necessary while they are occurring, but with this difference: that of the Sun could not fail to happen <note>The Sun's movement is an "absolute necessity" of nature.</note>, whereas that of the man was free <note>The man's movement is a "conditional necessity" based on his choice.</note>. Thus, what is present to God exists necessarily; but this necessity comes from their existence, even though this existence stems from the freedom of their causes. It is therefore not without reason that I said these things were necessary when referred to the knowledge of God, and free if they are considered in their own nature. Just as everything perceived by the senses is universal when viewed through the order of reason, even though in itself it is a particular thing. 

But what (you will say to me), if it is in my power to change my will, can I make this Providence <term>Providence</term> <note>From the Latin *providentia*, meaning "to see ahead." In this context, it refers to God's all-encompassing plan and gaze.</note> a liar by changing the object it knows? To this I respond: you can indeed take new resolutions, but because this Providence sees clearly that you can, and that you do so, it cannot fail. It is just as impossible to hide yourself from an eye that watches you, even though you freely take a thousand different postures. And what? Will this prescience <term>Prescience</term> <note>Foreknowledge; the quality of knowing something before it happens.</note> change according to my whim, and will God be obliged to

<summary>This page resolves the paradox of free will by arguing that God’s foreknowledge does not force human actions, but simply perceives them as they are chosen. It uses the analogy of an observer watching a person move to show that seeing an action doesn't make it involuntary.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the final book of Boethius’s *The Consolation of Philosophy*. It resolves the paradox of divine foreknowledge and human free will by explaining that God exists outside of time. <page-num>160</page-num>
<header>The Consolation</header>

to take up new thoughts as many times as I shall form new designs? By no means; for the divine intelligence beholds the whole future at once, without any shifting or succession of knowledge, but in a single glance, it anticipates all your changes without changing itself. This it possesses from the simplicity of its being, and not from the nature of future things. From this you may solve the difficulty you raised earlier, where it seemed unworthy to you that our events should be the cause of God's knowledge. For the power <term>vertu; here meaning the inherent capacity or strength</term> of this knowledge, gathering everything into its present notion, gives order to all things without taking anything from their sequence. 

This being so, the freedom of man remains entirely whole, and the Laws are not unjust in the distribution of punishments and rewards. And God, looking down upon us from Heaven as from a watchtower <note>original: "eſchauguette." This is a famous Boethian metaphor: God sits in a high tower looking down on the "road" of time. He sees the whole road at once, while we only see what is right in front of us.</note>, and matching His eternal vision with the outcome of our actions, grants rewards to virtue and punishments to crimes. Thus the confidence we place in His goodness, and the prayers we address to Him, cannot be useless when they are just. 

Therefore, flee vice, love virtue, raise your thoughts to high things, and lower your spirit only to the humility of prayer. *You are under a strict obligation to do well (unless you would maliciously feign ignorance), since you perform all your actions before the eyes of a God who sees all.* <note>This concluding sentence serves as a moral "call to action," suggesting that even if we cannot fully grasp the mechanics of eternity, the knowledge that we are being watched should drive us to live ethically.</note>

From this you may solve the difficulty you raised earlier, where it seemed unworthy to you that our events should be the cause of God's knowledge. For the power <term>power: here used in the sense of the original French 'vertu', meaning an inherent capacity or strength</term> of this knowledge, gathering everything into its present notion, gives order to all things without taking anything from their sequence. 

This being so, the freedom of man remains entirely whole, and the Laws are not unjust in the distribution of punishments and rewards. And God, looking down upon us from Heaven as from a watchtower <note>original: "eſchauguette." This is a famous Boethian metaphor: God sits in a high tower looking down on the "road" of time. He sees the whole landscape of history at once, whereas we, traveling the road, see only what is immediately in front of us.</note>, sees everything that is to happen, and the ever-present eternity of His vision keeps pace with the future quality of our actions, dispensing rewards to...

<summary>Lady Philosophy explains that God’s knowledge does not change when we change our minds, because He sees all temporal shifts from a single, eternal vantage point. This perspective preserves human free will and justifies divine justice, as God observes our actions from a metaphorical "watchtower" of eternity.</summary>

<keywords>Boethius, free will, divine foreknowledge, eternity, justice, watchtower metaphor, Providence, Philosophy</keywords>

# ON THE DEMON-MANIA OF WITCHES. <note>original: "De la Démonomanie des Sorciers." This work served as both a legal treatise and a theological warning, significantly influencing the witch trials of the 16th and 17th centuries.</note>
## BY J. BODIN OF ANGERS. <note>Jean Bodin (c. 1530–1596) was a prominent French jurist, political philosopher, and member of the Parlement of Paris, best known for his theories on sovereignty.</note>
### TO MY LORD MASTER CHRISTOPHE DE THOU, Knight, Lord of Cély, First President of the Court of the Parliament of Paris, and Counselor to the King in his Private Council. <note>Christophe de Thou was a powerful judicial figure and the father of the historian Jacques-Auguste de Thou. Bodin dedicated the work to him to seek legal legitimacy for his harsh views on prosecuting sorcery.</note>

->AT PARIS,<-

->At the house of JACQUES DU PUYS, Sworn Bookseller, at the sign of the Samaritan.<-

->1580.<-

->WITH THE KING’S PRIVILEGE.<-

<term>Demon-mania</term>: A term used by Bodin to describe the "madness" or obsession involving demons; it suggests both the crimes of the witches and the urgent need for society to obsess over their eradication.
<term>Angevin</term>: A descriptor indicating Bodin was from the French province of Anjou or the city of Angers.
<term>Sworn Bookseller</term>: <note>original: "Libraire juré"</note> A publisher officially licensed and sworn in by the University of Paris, signifying a high degree of professional and legal standing.
<term>King’s Privilege</term>: <note>original: "Privilege du Roy"</note> An early form of copyright protection where the monarch granted a printer the exclusive right to produce a specific book for a set number of years.

<summary>This is the title page for the first edition of Jean Bodin’s seminal work on demonology, establishing the author’s credentials and the high-ranking legal officials to whom the work was dedicated. It marks the beginning of one of the most significant legal justifications for the Great European Witch Hunt.</summary>
<keywords>Jean Bodin, witchcraft, demonology, legal history, 16th century, Paris, Christophe de Thou, publishing history</keywords>

## **Key Concepts**

<term>Digitization</term> <note>The technical process of scanning physical pages to create digital images and searchable text, ensuring historical documents are protected from physical decay and shared globally.</note>

<term>Preservation</term> <note>The practice of protecting historical materials from damage or loss; here, it refers to the digital "archiving" of books to ensure their content survives even if the physical paper deteriorates.</note>

<term>Google Books</term> <note>A massive digital repository and search engine that provides access to millions of scanned books from library collections, facilitating academic research and public access to knowledge.</note>

<term>Reproduction</term> <note>A copy of an original work; in this context, it refers to the digital surrogate created to represent the physical volume found in a library.</note>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the primary text following the digital preservation notice. It is the title page and opening preface of Franz Mesmer’s foundational work on his controversial medical theories.</meta>

### DISSERTATION ON THE DISCOVERY OF ANIMAL MAGNETISM.

->BY MR. MESMER, DOCTOR OF MEDICINE OF THE FACULTY OF VIENNA.<-

*AT GENEVA; AND TO BE FOUND IN PARIS,*

At P. FR. DIDOT the younger, Bookseller-Printer to MONSIEUR <note>In the French court of this era, "Monsieur" was the official title given to the eldest brother of the King. At the time of this publication in 1779, it referred to the Count of Provence, the brother of Louis XVI.</note>, Quai des Augustins.

->1779.<-

#### PREFACE.

Honesty is a sentiment that belongs to strong souls; others possess only politeness. <margin>Maxims of the author.</margin>

It is a custom as old as the Sciences and the Arts to see their progress accompanied by systems that serve as <term>milestones<note>original: "jalons". In a scientific or surveying context, these are stakes or markers used to chart a path or measure progress toward a goal.</note></term> for discoveries. Such a system is that of <term>Animal Magnetism<note>Franz Mesmer’s theory of an invisible natural force (a "fluid") possessed by all living beings, which he believed could be harnessed and manipulated to treat various illnesses.</note></term>.

Although truth is simple in itself, it is not always shared by every mind. <insert>It is often obscured by prejudices.</insert>

### DISSERTATION ON THE DISCOVERY OF ANIMAL MAGNETISM. <note>original: "Mémoire sur la découverte du magnétisme animal." This is the definitive work where Mesmer first formally presented his theories to the French scientific community.</note>

->BY MR. MESMER, DOCTOR OF MEDICINE OF THE FACULTY OF VIENNA.<-

*AT GENEVA; AND TO BE FOUND IN PARIS,*

At P. FR. DIDOT the younger, Bookseller-Printer to MONSIEUR <note>In the French court of this era, "Monsieur" was the official title given to the eldest brother of the King. At the time of this publication in 1779, it referred to the Count of Provence, the future Louis XVIII.</note>, Quai des Augustins.

->1779.<-

#### PREFACE.

Honesty is a sentiment that belongs to strong souls; others possess only politeness. <margin>Maxims of the author.</margin>

It is a custom as old as the Sciences and the Arts to see their progress accompanied by systems that serve as <term>milestones<note>original: "jalons". In a scientific or surveying context, these are stakes or markers used to chart a path or measure progress toward a goal.</note></term> for discoveries. Such a system is that of <term>Animal Magnetism<note>Franz Mesmer’s theory of an invisible natural force—a "fluid"—possessed by all living beings, which he believed could be harnessed and manipulated to treat various illnesses. This theory eventually evolved into the practice of mesmerism and later influenced the development of modern hypnotism.</note></term>.

Although truth is simple in itself, it is not always shared by every mind. <insert>It is often obscured by prejudices.</insert>
